                             VP ̸= VNP OVER EVERY FIELD

                                                     CLAUDE
                                               ANTHROPIC


        Abstract. We prove that every arithmetic circuit that computes the permanent of an
                                                               1/3
        𝑚 × 𝑚 matrix over the reals has more than 𝑚𝑐(log 𝑚)        gates, for some constant 𝑐 > 0 and
        all sufficiently large 𝑚. The same bound holds over every field of characteristic other than
        two and, for the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial in place of the permanent, the same bound
        holds over every field. Hence VP ̸= VNP over every field. This is the algebraic analogue of
        the conjecture P ̸= NP, but it does not settle that conjecture. Algebraic circuits must work
        for all inputs in the field and its extensions – not just 0 and 1 – thus our superpolynomial
        bound does not apply to Boolean circuits.
            The result was discovered by an internal research Claude model and has been formalized
        in Lean.




                                           1. Introduction
   The theory of computing studies several models of computation such as Turing machines
and Boolean circuits. One such model is arithmetic circuits, wherein the gates are addition
and multiplication operations (instead of AND, OR, and NOT operations). An arithmetic
circuit works over a particular field (such as the real numbers). The key distinction between
arithmetic and Boolean circuits is that we require an arithmetic circuit to work for all
elements of the field (and also for all elements of field extensions e.g. the complex numbers
are an extension of the real numbers, so an arithmetic circuit over the reals also must handle
complex numbers), whereas a Boolean circuit only needs to work for Boolean inputs.
   The famous P vs NP problem is a statement about Turing machines. The analogous
problem for arithmetic circuits is the VP vs VNP problem. In this paper, we resolve the
latter problem, but not the former.
   It has been shown that proving VP = VNP over an appropriate field would imply that
P/poly = NP/poly, which is the version of P = NP for Boolean circuits [Bür00b], but no
implication in the other direction is known. We show that VP ̸= VNP over all fields, but
the P vs NP problem remains open.
   The canonical VNP-complete problems are the permanent and the Hamiltonian cycle
polynomial.1 For an 𝑚 × 𝑚 matrix of variables 𝑥𝑖,𝑗 (𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ {1, 2, . . . , 𝑚}) these are the
multilinear polynomials
                                    𝑚
                                 ∑︁ ∏︁                                       𝑚
                                                                          ∑︁ ∏︁
                  PERM𝑚 =                  𝑥𝑖,𝜎(𝑖)         and   HC𝑚 =              𝑥𝑖,𝜎(𝑖) ,
                                𝜎∈𝑆𝑚 𝑖=1                                    𝜎 𝑖=1

where 𝑆𝑚 is the symmetric group of size 𝑚! and the second sum runs over the (𝑚 − 1)! cyclic
permutations in 𝑆𝑚 .
   The VP vs VNP problem is equivalent to asking whether or not the permanent and
the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial can be computed by polynomial-size arithmetic cir-
cuits; Valiant [Val79, Val82] conjectured that they cannot. We produce a lower bound of
          4/3
2Ω((log 𝑚) ) , which is superpolynomial and thus separates VP from VNP. Our lower bound
is quasipolynomial, whereas the best upper bounds are exponential – i.e. of the form 2𝑂(𝑚)
[Rys63, Gly10, Cur26]. Thus there is still plenty of room to tighten the bounds.
   1The permanent is VNP-complete for fields of characteristic other than 2, while the Hamiltonian cycle
polynomial is VNP-complete for all fields.

2                                           CLAUDE

  The full proofs are in the associated Lean release and in the appendix; a name in typewriter
type, such as thm_alt, refers to the relevant statement in Lean.
1.1. Results. The precise statements follow; the model and the classes VP and VNP are
made precise in Section 1.3.
Theorem 1.1 (main theorem; thm_allfields). There is an absolute constant 𝑐 > 0 such
that for every 𝑝, zero or prime, there is 𝑚0 with the following property. Over every field of
characteristic 𝑝 and for every 𝑚 ≥ 𝑚0 , every circuit for HC𝑚 , and every circuit for PERM𝑚
                                  1/3
if 𝑝 ̸= 2, has more than 𝑚𝑐(log 𝑚) gates.
  Here and throughout, log = log2 . We do not claim that the exponent 1/3 is optimal. In
characteristic two the permanent coincides with the determinant; since the determinant has
polynomial-size circuits, the theorem does not apply there.
  As (PERM𝑚 ) and (HC𝑚 ) are in VNP, Theorem 1.1 gives the following.
Corollary 1.2 (cor_allclasses). Over every field, VP is a proper subset of VNP.
  These results extend to approximate computation in the sense of border complexity, so
that VNP ̸⊆ VP over every field, and Section 1.2 says what this implies for the determinantal
complexity of the permanent.
Definition 1.3 (def_approx). Let k be a field, and let k((𝜀)) be the field of formal Laurent
series over k. A circuit over k((𝜀)) approximates a polynomial 𝑓 over k if it computes a
polynomial of the form 𝑓 + 𝜀𝑔, where 𝑔 is a polynomial all of whose coefficients lie in k[[𝜀]].
The class VP is defined like VP, with approximating circuits in place of circuits.
   Over the real numbers this captures limits. If the constants of the circuit are, say, poly-
nomials in 𝜀 and 1/𝜀, then putting a small positive real number for 𝜀 gives a circuit over
R with the same number of gates that computes 𝑓 + 𝜀𝑔, and these polynomials tend to 𝑓 ,
coefficient by coefficient, as 𝜀 → 0. So 𝑓 is a limit of polynomials that have small circuits,
although it need not have a small circuit itself, because constants such as 1/𝜀 blow up in the
limit. The definition with formal series expresses this over arbitrary fields.
Theorem 1.4 (approximate computation; thm_approx). Theorem 1.1 holds for circuits that
approximate HC𝑚 , respectively PERM𝑚 . In particular VNP ̸⊆ VP over every field.
   Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2 are proved in Appendix M (as Theorem M.3 and Corol-
lary M.4), and Theorem 1.4 in Appendix N (Definition N.1, Theorem N.5). Section 3 prints
the Lean file that states Theorems 1.1 and 1.4 and Corollary 1.2 from Mathlib alone.
1.2. Related work. The determinant of an 𝑚 × 𝑚 matrix can be computed with a number
of additions and multiplications that is polynomial in 𝑚 [Ber84]. Valiant’s conjecture says
that this is not so for the permanent, which differs from the determinant only in the signs of
its terms. For background on algebraic complexity theory we refer to the books and surveys
[BCS97, Bür00, SY10, Lan17, Sap21, Bür24].
   Lower bounds for general arithmetic circuits, that is, arithmetic circuits with no restriction
on their depth or structure, have been hard to obtain. To our knowledge, the best previous
lower bound for an explicit polynomial of degree 𝑑 in 𝑛 variables is the bound Ω(𝑛 log 𝑑) of
Strassen and of Baur and Strassen [Str73, BS83]. In particular, no lower bound of larger
order than 𝑚2 log 𝑚 was known for PERM𝑚 , which has 𝑚2 variables and degree 𝑚. Much
stronger bounds are known for restricted classes of circuits, and we review them next.
   For circuits over noncommutative rings, Raz recently proved that an explicit polynomial of
degree 𝑛 in 𝑛 variables needs Ω(𝑛1.5 ) product gates [Raz26]. For circuits over a field, bounds
stronger than Ω(𝑛 log 𝑑) are known only for restricted models. For formulas and algebraic
branching programs they are polynomial, at most quadratic in the number of variables
[Kal85, CKSV22]. Exponential lower bounds are known for monotone circuits [JS82, Yeh19]
and for noncommutative formulas and branching programs [Nis91], and bounds that are
                                          VP ̸= VNP                                          3

exponential in the square root of the degree for homogeneous circuits of depth four, first with
bounded bottom fan-in [GKKS14] and then in general [KLSS17, KS17]. Superpolynomial
lower bounds are known for multilinear formulas [Raz09] and, by a breakthrough of Limaye,
Srinivasan and Tavenas, for circuits of every constant depth in characteristic zero or large
characteristic [LST25], which Forbes extended to all fields [For24]. Many of these proofs
bound the rank of a matrix of partial derivatives or of coefficients [Nis91, NW97]. Depth
reduction, from [Hya79, VSBR83] to the reductions to depth four and three [AV08, Koi12,
Tav15, GKKS16], connects these models to general circuits. Raz related lower bounds for
general circuits to elusive functions [Raz10] and lower bounds for formulas to tensor rank
[Raz13]. In comparison, Theorem 1.1 concerns general circuits, with no restriction on their
depth, on their constants or on cancellations, and its bound is superpolynomial, whereas the
bound of Baur and Strassen is linear in the number of variables up to a logarithmic factor.
   Geometric complexity theory, the programme of Mulmuley and Sohoni [MS01, MS08,
BLMW11], approaches the border version of the conjecture through representation theory:
it seeks representations that obstruct the padded permanent from lying in the orbit closure
of a determinant of polynomial size. Many known lower bounds fit its framework [Gro15],
and occurrence obstructions, on which the original plan relied, cannot give superpolynomial
bounds [IP17, BIP19]. Our proof does not follow this programme, since it uses neither rep-
resentation theory nor orbit closures (Section 2), but it bears on the statement at which
the programme aims: the bound for approximating circuits (Theorem 1.4) implies that
the permanent is not approximated by determinants of polynomial size, which over C was
conjectured by Mulmuley and Sohoni [MS01] in the language of orbit closures (this conse-
quence is the one numbered statement of this paper that is not formalized in Lean). For
the determinantal complexity of the 𝑚 × 𝑚 permanent the classical lower bound is qua-
dratic in 𝑚 [MR04, CCL10, Yab15], also for the border version [LMR13]. In 2026 a cubic
lower bound, also for the border version over C, was announced in a manuscript of OpenAI
that comes with a Lean development [OAI26], and it was extended to odd characteristic in
[She26b]; from the same manuscript, [Guo26] derives a bound of order 𝑚2 log 𝑚 for circuits
with division. For the power sum 𝑥𝑛1 + · · · + 𝑥𝑛𝑛 a bound that is quadratic in the number
of variables was announced in [She26], and a short proof for determinantal complexity is
given in [KV26]. Border complexity goes back to [BCRL79, Ald84], and for the class VP,
for border reductions and for closures of small classes we refer to [Bür04, GMQ16, IS22] and
[BIZ18, Kum20, DDS21].
   Several barriers are known for natural strategies towards lower bounds, and we comment
briefly on how our proof, which is outlined in Section 2, relates to them. Rank methods
cannot give strong lower bounds for the rank of tensors [EGOW18, GMOW19]. In the flat
case we use, instead of ranks, functionals in a variant of Strassen’s asymptotic spectrum
[Str88, WZ26], which we call spectral points. Few points of the asymptotic spectrum are
known explicitly, namely the ranks of flattenings [Str88], the support functionals on oblique
tensors [Str91] and, over C, the quantum functionals [CVZ23]. The spectral points that
we use are not explicit at all, since they are only known to exist by Zorn’s lemma (prop_
gauge). Algebraically natural proofs [FSV18, GKSS17], the analogue of the natural proofs
of Razborov and Rudich [RR97], may be unable to prove superpolynomial lower bounds
for general circuits, and there is evidence in both directions [CKRST20, KRST22]. In the
contracting case no property of polynomials other than low degree is used, and the argu-
ment ends in a diagonalization. We have not analysed how it relates to relativization and
algebrization [BGS75, AW09].
   Lower bounds for the permanent have consequences for the derandomization of polynomial
identity testing [KI04], but we have not worked out what our bound gives there.

1.3. The model. We now make the model precise. An arithmetic circuit over a commutative
ring 𝑅 is a finite list of polynomials over 𝑅, each of which is a variable, a constant from 𝑅,
or the sum or the product of two earlier entries of the list, which may be the same entry.
4                                                 CLAUDE

We call the entries of the list the gates of the circuit, and we say that the circuit computes
each of the polynomials in the list. The size of the circuit is the length of the list, so that
variables and constants are counted as gates.2
   Valiant’s classes are defined as follows [Val79, Val82]. A family (𝑓𝑛 ) of polynomials over
a field is in VP if the number of variables of 𝑓𝑛 , its degree, and the size of the smallest
circuit that  computes it are all bounded by polynomials in 𝑛. The family is in VNP if
𝑓𝑛 (𝑥) = 𝑒∈{0,1}𝑟(𝑛) 𝑔𝑛 (𝑥, 𝑒) for some family (𝑔𝑛 ) in VP. Clearly VP ⊆ VNP, and Valiant’s
          ∑︀

conjecture is the statement that VP ̸= VNP. The family (PERM𝑚 ) belongs to VNP, for
example by Ryser’s formula [Rys63]. As said above, it is complete for VNP over every field
in which 2 ̸= 0; this is a theorem of Valiant [Val79].
   We said above that an arithmetic circuit has to work for all elements of the field and of its
extensions. Equivalently, the circuit produces the polynomial itself, as a formal expression
over the field, by additions and multiplications only, and not merely its values on inputs
from {0, 1}. A Turing machine or a Boolean circuit, in contrast, may manipulate the bits
of its input in any way. The results of this paper therefore say nothing about whether NP-
complete problems have fast algorithms. (Evaluating the permanent of a matrix with entries
0 and 1 is #P-complete [Val79b], but that is a statement about Boolean computation.) As
said above, it is not known whether Valiant’s conjecture implies Boolean separations such
as P ̸= NP, while in the other direction a separation of Boolean circuit classes is known
to imply Valiant’s conjecture over appropriate fields. For example, if NP ̸⊆ P/poly, then
VP ̸= VNP over C, provided that the Riemann hypothesis for Dedekind zeta functions holds
[Bür00b].

                                     2. Overview of the proof
  This section describes the proof informally; Section 2.5 says why the argument applies
neither to the determinant nor to Boolean circuits. Throughout this section we assume
that the field of coefficients k has characteristic different from two; the last paragraph of
Section 2.5 treats characteristic two.

2.1. The shape of the argument. The proof consists of two cases, the contracting case
and the flat case, according to which of two technical hypotheses (described next) holds.
Both are hypotheses on the field k.

        Figure 1. The structure of the proof. The two arrows marked P are the
        only steps that use the permanent; they use the circuit too, but only by
        substituting into it. The two arrows marked C are the only steps that look
        inside the circuit, both times at the polynomial at every gate. In the shaded
        boxes neither the polynomial that is to be computed nor a circuit occurs; the
        dashed boxes are the assumptions. For the determinant the arrows P are
        missing, and a Boolean circuit does not pass the arrows C (Section 2.5).

Flat hypothesis: For every finite subset 𝑉 of a Euclidean space and every 𝜀 > 0 there are
    a spectral point 𝜑 on 𝑉 and a real number 𝑐 ≥ 0 such that |𝑐 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢, 𝑣) − ‖𝑢 − 𝑣‖| ≤ 𝜀 for
    all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 .
   The field enters only through the tensors, which have entries in k, and the restrictions,
which use k-linear maps. The set Mat𝜇 (F2 ) only supplies the parties and their addition; its
F2 is not the field k. A price on 𝑉 is a pointwise limit of functions 𝑐 𝑑𝜑 with real numbers
𝑐 ≥ 0 (def_price); we write |𝑢𝑣|, or |𝑢, 𝑣|, for the distance of 𝑢 and 𝑣 in a given price, and
for a price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) we write 𝐷(𝑟)2 for the mean of |𝑎, 𝑎 + 𝑊 |2 over all 𝑎 and all 𝑊
of rank 𝑟. The Lean definition states both hypotheses for prices: the contracting one as
𝐷(𝜇) ≤ 𝑠 𝐷(1) for all prices on Mat𝜇 (F2 ), which is the same because the inequality survives
scaling and limits, and the flat one as the statement that all finite subsets of Euclidean
spaces, with their metrics, are prices.
                                          √
   Every price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) has 𝐷(𝜇) ≤ 𝜇 𝐷(1). Indeed, for 𝑊, 𝑊 ′ of ranks 𝑖, 𝑗 whose sum
has rank 𝑖 + 𝑗, the points 𝑎, 𝑎 + 𝑊 , 𝑎 + 𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ , 𝑎 + 𝑊 ′ form a quadrilateral with two sides
𝑊 , two sides 𝑊 ′ and, since 2 = 0, both diagonals 𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ ; prices have nonpositive curvature
(Section 2.2), where the squared diagonals of a quadrilateral add up to at most its squared
sides (lem_toolkit_b), with equality for rectangles in a Euclidean space. So the contracting
hypothesis says that, for some 𝜇, every price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) falls short of a Euclidean rectangle
by a fixed factor, and the flat hypothesis says that prices can be Euclidean.
6                                           CLAUDE

Theorem 2.2 (the two cases; thm_alt). Over every field k at least one of the two hypotheses
of Definition 2.1 holds, that is, one of the two cases occurs.
   We do not know which of the two hypotheses holds, even over R, nor do we show that the
two are mutually exclusive.
   Rather than analyzing the permanent or Hamiltonian cycle polynomial directly, in each
case we work with another polynomial, which the permanent, and likewise the Hamiltonian
cycle polynomial, can express. In the flat case we prove a lower bound (prop_flat_rate)
for the polynomial of a network of wires on the complete graph, which for 𝑘 ≥ 2 and ℓ ≥ 1 is

                                               ∑︁ ∏︁
(2.1)                                 𝑓𝑘,ℓ =            𝑥𝑎,𝜎𝑎 .
                                               𝜎 𝑎=1

Here 𝜎 = (𝜎𝑎𝑏 )𝑎<𝑏 assigns a string 𝜎𝑎𝑏 = 𝜎𝑏𝑎 ∈ {0, 1}ℓ to each edge 𝑎𝑏 of the complete graph
on the nodes 1, . . . , 𝑘, and the sum is over all 2ℓ𝑘(𝑘−1)/2 such assignments. For a node 𝑎
we write 𝜎𝑎 = (𝜎𝑎𝑏 )𝑏̸=𝑎 for the 𝑘 − 1 strings on its edges. The variables are the 𝑥𝑎,𝜌 with
𝑎 a node and 𝜌 ∈ ({0, 1}ℓ )𝑘−1 , so each term picks, for every node, the variable indexed by
the strings on its edges, and the choices at two nodes are tied together by the string on
the edge between them. In the contracting case we refute a small circuit (lem_circuit,
thm_noacc) for a polynomial whose values are the sums over the weighted runs of all short
nondeterministic programs, namely
                                            ∑︁
(2.2)                                  Φ=          𝑈𝑡 (𝑤) 𝑦 𝑤 .
                                               𝑤

Here 𝑤 runs over the possible inputs of the automaton, that is, the strings (words) of fewer
than 𝑛 symbols from its finite alphabet, 𝑈𝑡 (𝑤) is the value of a fixed universal automaton on
𝑤 in 𝑡 steps (Section 2.4, which also fixes 𝑛 and 𝑡), there is one variable 𝑦𝑖,𝑐 for each place 𝑖
and each 𝑐 that is a symbol or the end mark, and 𝑦 𝑤 is the product of the variables 𝑦𝑖,𝑤𝑖 for
the symbols of 𝑤 and of the variable of the end mark at the place after its last symbol; the
point that encodes 𝑤 has these coordinates equal to 1 and all others equal to 0.
Where the permanent enters. It enters once in each case (lem_fkl_perm and lem_circuit),
both times through an explicit form of Valiant’s completeness theorem [Val79]: both 𝑓𝑘,ℓ
and Φ are clause sums (that is, sums over all bit vectors of products of local weights), so
a circuit for the permanent gives, by substitution, circuits of about the same size for them.
The Hamiltonian cycle polynomial has the same property, while no such reduction is known
for the determinant.
Where the circuit enters. These two steps use the circuit only by substituting into it, which
any device that computes the same values would allow. Two other steps, one in each case,
look inside the circuit, at the polynomial that each gate computes as a formal expression. In
the flat case, a decomposition lemma (lem_balanced) splits the polynomial at every gate,
and so writes the tensor of the coefficients of the output as a sum of terms of rank at most the
number 𝐿 of gates (Section 2.2). In the contracting case, an evaluation lemma (lem_lowdeg)
evaluates the part of degree at most 𝑑 of the output, at the points with coordinates 0 and
1 that encode finite words, the inputs of the programs of Section 2.4, in about 𝑑 (log 𝐿)𝑂(1)
steps, which in our application is far fewer than 𝐿. It can do so because the evaluator is a
nondeterministic program whose value is a sum over its runs, one run for each parse tree
[JS82, MP08] with at most 𝑑 leaves, and because a run does not compute with the constants
of the circuit but only records their names (Step 5 in Section 2.4).

2.2. The common part. This section sets up the tools that both cases use. The basic one
turns ranks into distances. A tensor of rank at most 𝑟 across a split of its indices into two
parts is a restriction of a network in which every index is wired to one of two hubs and the
two hubs are joined by a wire of dimension 𝑟. The value of a spectral point 𝜑 on a network
of wires is known: its logarithm is the sum, over the wires, of the size of the wire times the
                                                     VP ̸= VNP                                             7

distance 𝑑𝜑 between its two ends, which we call the length of the network. So log 𝜑(𝑡) is at
most the length of the wires to the hubs plus log 𝑟 times the distance between the hubs (2.3).
   This is how a circuit enters in the flat case. A polynomial 𝑓 that is linear in each of 𝑘
blocks of variables has a tensor of coefficients with one index for each block, and a circuit
with 𝐿 gates for 𝑓 writes 𝑓 as a sum of at most 2𝑘 pieces, each of rank at most 𝐿 across a
split of the blocks. If 𝑓 is itself a network of wires, then log 𝜑(𝑓 ) is exactly its length, and
the comparison (2.4) shows that log 𝐿 must be large whenever some spectral point makes
this network long, the wires to the hubs short, and the two hubs close to each other.
   Which metrics 𝑑𝜑 are available is a question of geometry. The inequality (2.3), applied
to two crossing wires, shows that these metrics always have nonpositive curvature, and
within nonpositive curvature the two hypotheses of Definition 2.1 are the two possibilities
(Theorem 2.2). In the flat case, Euclidean metrics are available and (2.4) gives the bound
directly (Section 2.3). In the contracting case the geometry is used differently: inequalities
between lengths that hold for all spectral points turn, by a duality, into identities between
tensors (Section 2.4).
From circuits to lengths. Recall the spectral points 𝜑, the wires and the metrics 𝑑𝜑 of Defi-
nition 2.1. A spectral point evaluates a product of wires exactly: if 𝑡 is the product of wires
⟨𝑞𝑒 ⟩𝑢𝑒 𝑣𝑒 , then multiplicativity
                   ∑︀
                                   and the value 𝑞 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢,𝑣) of 𝜑 on a single wire (lem_exponents)
give log 𝜑(𝑡) = 𝑒 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢𝑒 , 𝑣𝑒 ) log 𝑞𝑒 , the sum over the wires of the distance between the two
ends times the size of the wire. We call this sum the length of 𝑡 in the metric 𝑑𝜑 . Products of
wires are the only tensors whose value can be read off from the metric 𝑑𝜑 . The spectral points
that the argument uses are not explicit (they come from the hypotheses of Definition 2.1),
so for a general tensor, such as that of the permanent, we have no formula for 𝜑(𝑡), only the
upper bound from ranks that follows. This is why the argument works with polynomials
whose tensors are products of wires.
   Ranks become distances (lem_hubs): a tensor 𝑡 of rank at most 𝑟 across a partition of the
parties into 𝐼1 and 𝐼2 is a restriction of a product of wires that join every party 𝑝 of 𝐼𝑖 to a
hub 𝑜𝑖 with the dimension 𝑛𝑝 of the index of 𝑝, and the hubs to each other with dimension 𝑟
(Figure 2, right). Hence, for every spectral point 𝜑 on the parties and the hubs,
                          ∑︁                            ∑︁
(2.3)        log 𝜑(𝑡) ≤          𝑑𝜑 (𝑝, 𝑜1 ) log 𝑛𝑝 +          𝑑𝜑 (𝑝, 𝑜2 ) log 𝑛𝑝 + 𝑑𝜑 (𝑜1 , 𝑜2 ) log 𝑟,
                          𝑝∈𝐼1                          𝑝∈𝐼2

where the rank enters only through the last term.
   Now let 𝑓 be linear in each of 𝑘 blocks of variables of equal size, so that its coefficients form
a tensor with one party for each block, and∑︀call a set 𝑆 of blocks balanced if 𝑘/3 ≤ |𝑆| ≤ 2𝑘/3.
If 𝑓 has a circuit with 𝐿 gates, then 𝑓 = 𝑆 𝑓𝑆 over the balanced sets, where the tensor of
𝑓𝑆 has rank at most 𝐿 across 𝑆 (lem_balanced, as in depth reduction [Hya79, VSBR83]).
With hubs 𝑜𝑆 , 𝑜′𝑆 for every balanced set, a spectral point has 𝜑(𝑓 ) ≤ 2𝑘 max𝑆 𝜑(𝑓𝑆 ), and
(2.3) with 𝑟 = 𝐿 bounds 𝜑(𝑓𝑆 ). If 𝑓 is itself a product of wires, the left-hand side is known
8                                              CLAUDE

too, and for some balanced set 𝑆
(2.4)     length of 𝑓 ≤ 𝑘 + length of the wires to the hubs of 𝑆 + 𝑑𝜑 (𝑜𝑆 , 𝑜′𝑆 ) log 𝐿.
So log 𝐿 is large if one spectral point has, for all balanced sets at once, hubs that are close to
each other while the wires to them are shorter than 𝑓 by more than the 𝑘 bits that name a
balanced set. We therefore need a polynomial that is a product of wires, admits such hubs,
and is expressed by the permanent.
Curvature. The geometry of prices decides which hubs exist, and it is what separates the two
cases. The product of two crossing wires, between 𝑎 and 𝑐 and between 𝑏 and 𝑑 (Figure 2,
left), has full rank across both partitions 𝑎𝑏 | 𝑐𝑑 and 𝑎𝑑 | 𝑏𝑐, so (2.3) says nothing about it.
An identity between integer tensors (prop_identity) writes it as a sum of few terms, each of
small rank across one partition when its rank across the other is large, and (2.3), applied to
the largest term once for each partition, gives an inequality between the distances among the
four ends and the four hubs (prop_crossing). Averaging it over fine chains of points gives
the inequality of Bruhat and Tits [BT72] for prices (thm_cn): |𝑧𝑦|2 ≤ 21 |𝑧𝑥|2 + 12 |𝑧𝑥′ |2 − 14 |𝑥𝑥′ |2
for all points 𝑥, 𝑥′ , 𝑧 and every midpoint 𝑦 of 𝑥 and 𝑥′ , with equality in Euclidean spaces. A
complete metric space in which any two points have a midpoint that satisfies it is a CAT(0)
space [BH99, Stu03], and every price is induced by a map into such a space, whose metric is
again a price (prop_random, prop_space).
The two cases. To prove Theorem 2.2, suppose that the contracting hypothesis of Defini-
                                                                                           √
tion 2.1 fails; then a compactness argument gives prices on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) with 𝐷(𝜇) = 𝜇 𝐷(1),
that is, with equality in the quadrilateral inequality, and the flat rectangle property of CAT(0)
spaces (lem_toolkit_f) puts a solid cube of R𝜇 , and so every finite subset of R𝜇 up to scale,
                                                        4/3
into a price. The flat case gives the bound 2𝑐(log 𝑚) of Theorem 1.1 with an explicit con-
                                                                                           2
stant 𝑐 (thm_flat_rate), and the contracting case gives the stronger bound 2𝑐(log 𝑚) / log log 𝑚
(thm_contracting). In the contracting case the constants, among them 𝑚0 , depend on the
integer 𝜇, for which the proof gives no value, and this is why Theorem 1.1 comes with no
value for 𝑚0 .

2.3. The flat case. The polynomial. For 𝑘 ≥ 2 and ℓ ≥ 1 the complete network is the
complete graph on the nodes 1, . . . , 𝑘 with a string of ℓ bits on each edge, and its polynomial
is the polynomial 𝑓𝑘,ℓ of (2.1). It is linear in each of 𝑘 blocks of variables, one for each node,
and the index of the party of a node consists of 𝑘 − 1 strings. We split this party into 𝑘 − 1
parties, the ends (𝑎, 𝑏) of the edges 𝑎𝑏 at 𝑎. This only regroups the indices, so ranks across
sets of nodes do not change, and (2.3) and (2.4) hold with the ends as the parties 𝑝 and with
log 𝑛𝑝 = ℓ. The tensor of 𝑓𝑘,ℓ is now the product, over the edges, of the wires of dimension
2ℓ between the ends (𝑎, 𝑏) and (𝑏, 𝑎). So its length is known, and every balanced set 𝑆 of
nodes is crossed by |𝑆|(𝑘 − |𝑆|) ≥ 2𝑘 2 /9 of the 𝑘(𝑘 − 1)/2 wires (Figure 3, left). It is also
a clause sum: its bits are those of 𝜎, and for every variable 𝑥𝑎,𝜌 a clause with this weight
says that 𝜎𝑎 = 𝜌. Hence a circuit with 𝐿′ gates for PERM𝑚 , where 𝑚 ≥ 3𝑘 3 ℓ 2𝑘ℓ , gives one
with 𝐿 = 5𝐿′ gates for 𝑓𝑘,ℓ (lem_fkl_perm), the only use of the permanent in this case, and
log 𝑚 is about 𝑘ℓ.
A Euclidean configuration. By the flat hypothesis we may choose positions in R𝑘 for the
ends and for the hubs of all balanced sets: for every 𝛿 > 0 some 𝑐 𝑑𝜑 differs from their
Euclidean distances by less than 𝛿, and this one 𝜑 serves all balanced sets at once. For 𝑎 < 𝑏
we put the end (𝑎, 𝑏) at 21 𝑒𝑎 and the end (𝑏, 𝑎) at − 21 𝑒𝑎 , so that every wire has length 1. As
at least 2𝑘 2 /9 wires cross a balanced set 𝑆, the positions of the ends (𝑎, 𝑏) with 𝑎 ∈ 𝑆 add
up to a vector of length at least 𝑘 3/2 /9, and the hubs of 𝑆 go at a distance 𝜂 of order 𝑘 −1/2
from the origin, in its direction and opposite to it. The distances from the ends to their
hubs then add up to less than the total length of the wires, the saving is a fixed fraction
of 𝑘, and (2.4), multiplied by 𝑐, becomes ℓ · saving ≤ 𝑐𝑘 + 2𝜂 log 𝐿 up to errors that vanish
with 𝛿. The term 𝑐𝑘 is negligible for fixed 𝑘 and large ℓ, because 𝜑 and 𝑐 were chosen for

𝑘 alone (prop_flat_lower, thm_flat_simple). For growing  √    𝑘 it is replaced by about 𝑘 3/2
(lem_few, prop_cube),

of order 𝑟4 = (log 𝑚)4/3 , this gives the bound of Theorem 1.1 in the flat case (thm_flat_
rate).

2.4. The contracting case. Here the argument is indirect. Steps 1 to 4 prove, with no cir-
cuit and no permanent, that every computation of a nondeterministic program with weighted
runs can be cut into short pieces, which a fast program lists. In Step 5 a small circuit for
the permanent would speed up the sum over the runs of every short piece, hence all such
computations, and a diagonal argument refutes this.
Steps 1 to 3: from contraction to trees. First, the contracting hypothesis yields a Poincaré
inequality for all prices on the groups 𝑈ℎ of upper unitriangular ℎ × ℎ matrices over F2 ,
again only sets of points: the mean square distance of two random elements is at most
a constant, the same for all multiples ℎ of a fixed integer, times the sum over the ℎ − 1
elementary generators of the mean square distances by which they move a point (thm_
poincare). Second, a form of Strassen’s duality (thm_duality) turns an inequality between
lengths of products of wires that holds in every price into an identity that makes a high tensor
power of one product from few restrictions of the others. From the Poincaré inequality it
makes the rerouting laws (thm_laws), whose coefficients generate a finitely generated ring 𝐴
and which hold over every commutative 𝐴-algebra. Third, the laws write the value of every
network of tensors joined by wires as a sum of values of tree networks, which can be cut into
subtrees joined by wires of small total size (thm_tree).
Step 4: computations can be cut. An instance 𝑧 is a nondeterministic program with an
argument, whose runs have weights in 𝐴, and sum(𝑧) is the sum of the weights of its accepting
runs. A run may also record names of constants in a list; it never computes with a constant,
and only Step 5 uses names. The instance is 𝜏 -plain if all its runs halt and the accepting ones
take at most 𝜏 steps and name no constants. A fixed universal machine, a tape automaton,
evaluates sum(𝑧) for plain 𝑧 (prop_universal), and its runs form networks (lem_runnet),
to which Step 3 applies. The result is the cutting theorem (thm_cut). Let 𝑣 be a large
integer, the scale, let 𝐻 be a suitable constant, and let 𝜏 ≥ 𝑣 𝐻 lie below a fixed power of 𝑣.
For every 𝜏 -plain instance 𝑧 of size at most 𝜏 /𝑣 48 , the sum sum(𝑧) is the sum, over the runs
of a program whose accepting runs take at most 𝜏 /𝑣 24 steps, of the weight of the run times
the product of sum(𝑦) over the pieces 𝑦 that the run lists. A run lists at most 𝜏 𝑣 2 /𝑣 𝐻 pieces,
and they are 𝑣 𝐻 -plain and short, that is, of size at most 𝑣 𝐻−32 .
Step 5: accelerators. Only now suppose that PERM𝑚 has a circuit with 𝑣 𝑎 gates, where 𝑚 is
a large power of 𝑣 and 𝑎 is any integer with (log 𝑣)𝑂(𝑎) ≤ 𝑣, so that the circuit may be much
10                                              CLAUDE

larger than 𝑣 𝐻 . The polynomial Φ of (2.2), with 𝑛 = 𝑣 𝐻−32 + 1 and 𝑡 = 𝑣 𝐻+1 , has degree at
most 𝑛, which is about the length of the input and not the number of steps, and takes the
value sum(𝑧) at the point with coordinates 0 and 1 that encodes a short 𝑣 𝐻 -plain 𝑧. It is
a clause sum with 𝑣 𝑂(1) literals, whose bits describe a word and, step by step, a run of the
automaton on it, so the reduction to the permanent gives a circuit with 𝑂(𝑣 𝑎 ) gates for Φ
(cor_clauses), the only use of the permanent in this case. Now the evaluation lemma looks
inside this circuit (lem_lowdeg, Section 2.1): the constants that a run of its program names
are coefficients that depend on the circuit and not on the input. The value of a program
whose runs name constants is the sum, over its accepting runs, of the weight of the run times
the constants that it names. In this sense the circuit gives an accelerator at the scale 𝑣 (def_
acc, lem_circuit): a program of size 𝑣 𝑂(𝑎+1) , with as many named constants, whose value
on every short 𝑣 𝐻 -plain 𝑧 is sum(𝑧) and whose accepting runs take at most 𝑣 𝐻−16 steps.
   At the scale 𝑣 this advice is longer than the running times, so we cannot diagonalize there.
A computation that contains runs of the accelerator names constants, so the cutting theorem
(thm_cut) does not apply to it. But the names can       be moved in front of the sum over the
runs (lem_postpone): the value is a combination 𝑒 𝜆𝑒 sum(𝑧𝑒 ) of sums of plain instances 𝑧𝑒 ,
                                                    ∑︀

with products 𝜆𝑒 of the constants as coefficients. Cutting the 𝑧𝑒 as in Step 4 and running the
accelerator on every piece turns 𝜏 steps into fewer than 𝜏 /𝑣 8 , and at most 3𝛼 such rounds
give a program that is again correct on all short plain instances and as fast, with the same
constants, at the scale 𝑣 𝛼 (prop_next). The 𝑣 𝑎 constants are short only when 𝛼 is of the
order of 𝑎, and the cutting theorem (thm_cut) then cuts 𝑂(𝛼) times in succession, each time
at the cost of a power of log 𝑣; this is the condition (log 𝑣)𝑂(𝑎) ≤ 𝑣, which limits the bound
in this case (thm_contracting). Correctness of an accelerator is a family of identities in its
constants over 𝐴, so it survives reduction to a finite field 𝐸 [Eis95, Theorem 4.19], in which
boundedly many names cover all powers of the constants. At a scale that is large compared
with 𝐸, let P be the program that on 𝑥 guesses a bit and either accepts with weight 1 or
applies the accelerator, its names again moved in front, to “𝑥 applied to 𝑥”. Applied to itself,
P has a value 𝑉 with 𝑉 = 1 + 𝑉 , which is impossible in a nonzero ring (thm_noacc).

2.5. The determinant and Boolean circuits. The determinant. The determinant is
very similar to the permanent, but has circuits of polynomial size [Ber84], so the proof must
fail for it, and it fails at one point only: the reduction of clause sums to the permanent (the
arrows marked P in Figure 1). So our results imply that 𝑓𝑘,ℓ in the flat case, and Φ in the
contracting case, has no reduction of comparable size to determinants.
Boolean circuits. A Boolean circuit for HC𝑚 amounts to an arithmetic circuit over F2 that
only has to be correct on           the inputs 0 and 1. So instead of HC𝑚 it may compute any
                                 ∑︀ 2
polynomial 𝑔 = HC𝑚 + 𝑖 (𝑥𝑖 − 𝑥𝑖 )ℎ𝑖 , and our theorem says nothing about such 𝑔. The
proof breaks at the two steps that look inside the circuit (marked C in Figure 1). The
decomposition lemma (lem_balanced) uses the part of the computed polynomial that is
linear in each block of variables, and the evaluation lemma (lem_lowdeg) uses its part of
low degree; for 𝑔 these parts need have nothing to do with those of HC𝑚 . For instance,
(𝑥21,1 − 𝑥1,1 ) 𝑥2,1 · · · 𝑥𝑘,1 vanishes at all inputs 0 and 1, yet its part that is linear in each block
is −𝑥1,1 𝑥2,1 · · · 𝑥𝑘,1 . The substitutions (marked P) are not the obstacle: they send inputs 0
and 1 to inputs 0 and 1 (cor_clauses_hc).
    Nor can a large Boolean circuit take the place of the program that the contracting case
refutes, since we know no way to run it faster than gate by gate.
The field. Which of the two hypotheses holds may depend on the field. Otherwise the field
only supplies the scalars: the crossing identity is between integer tensors, the geometry sees
only the real numbers 𝜑(𝑡), and the steps marked C use identities between polynomials that
hold over any commutative ring. Of the field we use only that 2 ̸= 0, for the matrix entries
± 12 in the construction (prop_grid) behind the reduction to the permanent (cor_clauses);
in characteristic two, where the permanent is the determinant, the construction stops there,
                                                               VP ̸= VNP                                           11

whereas the one for HC𝑚 needs no division (prop_hc_grid), so the reduction to HC𝑚 holds
over every field (cor_clauses_hc). Another field means other scalars for the same tensor,
whereas a circuit that is correct only on 0 and 1 computes another polynomial and hence
another tensor.

                                                  3. Lean Formalization
   The proofs in this paper were found and written by AI. No human mathematician has
checked the proofs. They have been formalized and verified in Lean 4 [Lean, Mathlib]. Every
numbered statement of this paper, in Sections 1 and 2 and in the appendix, has a Lean equiva-
lent proved from Mathlib and the standard axioms, with one exception: Remark N.6, the con-
sequence for determinantal complexity that Section 1.2 draws from Theorem 1.4. The Lean
proof assumes nothing beyond Mathlib. It is available at https://github.com/anthropics/
formal-math/tree/main/vp-vnp. The development uses Lean 4.33.1 and Mathlib at com-
mit 0df444a360e, contains no sorry outside Challenge.lean, which states the theorems
for checking, and uses no axioms other than propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound.
   We now print the file Statement.lean of the Lean development, which imports Mathlib
and nothing else and states the main results so that a reader can check that it correctly
formalizes the statements in this paper. The file Solution.lean proves them.
import Mathlib

/-!
# Superpolynomial lower bounds for the permanent and the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial

Definitions and statements, using Mathlib only. The proofs are in Solution.lean.
-/

open MvPolynomial

universe u

namespace Statement

/-- `f` has an arithmetic circuit with at most `s` gates: a list of `s` polynomials contains `f`, and each of
them is a variable, a constant, or the sum or product of two earlier ones (possibly the same one). Variables and
constants count as gates. -/
def Computes {R : Type u} {V : Type} [CommSemiring R] (f : MvPolynomial V R) (s : N) : Prop :=
  ∃ l : N → MvPolynomial V R,
    (∀ k < s, (∃ v : V, l k = X v) ∨ (∃ a : R, l k = C a) ∨
      (∃ i < k, ∃ j < k, l k = l i + l j) ∨ (∃ i < k, ∃ j < k, l k = l i * l j)) ∧
    ∃ k < s, l k = f

/-- The permanent of the generic `m × m` matrix. -/
noncomputable def perm (F : Type∏︀u) [CommSemiring F] (m : N) : MvPolynomial (Fin m × Fin m) F :=
      𝜎 : Equiv.Perm (Fin m),       i, X (𝜎 i, i)
  ∑︀

open Classical in
/-- The Hamiltonian cycle polynomial: the sum over the permutations with a single cycle through all points. -/
noncomputable def hc (F : Type u) [CommSemiring F] (m : N)∏︀: MvPolynomial (Fin m × Fin m) F :=
      𝜎 : Equiv.Perm (Fin m) with 𝜎.IsCycleOn Set.univ,       i, X (i, 𝜎 i)
  ∑︀

/-- More than `m ^ (c * (log2 m) ^ (1/3))` gates. -/
def Beyond (c : R) (m s : N) : Prop := (m : R) ^ (c * Real.logb 2 m ^ (1 / 3 : R)) < s

/-- **Main theorem.** There is an absolute constant `c > 0`, and for each characteristic `p` an `m0 `, such
that over every field of characteristic `p` and for every `m ≥ m0 ` every circuit for `hc m`, and for
`perm m` if `p ̸= 2`, has more than `m ^ (c * (log2 m) ^ (1/3))` gates. -/
def MainTheorem : Prop :=
  ∃ c : R, 0 < c ∧ ∀ p : N, ∃ m0 : N, ∀ (F : Type u) [Field F] [CharP F p], ∀ m ≥ m0 , ∀ s : N,
    (Computes (hc F m) s → Beyond c m s) ∧ (p ̸= 2 → Computes (perm F m) s → Beyond c m s)
12                                                             CLAUDE

/-! ### Approximate circuits -/

/-- `f` is approximated by a circuit with at most `s` gates: a circuit over the Laurent series in `𝜀`
computes some `q = f + 𝜀 * g`, where the coefficients of `g` are power series in `𝜀`. Here `a.coeff i` is
the coefficient of `𝜀 ^ i` in the series `a`. -/
def Approximates {F : Type u} {V : Type} [Field F] (f : MvPolynomial V F) (s : N) : Prop :=
  ∃ q : MvPolynomial V (LaurentSeries F), Computes q s ∧
    ∀ (d : V →0 N) (i : Z), i ≤ 0 → (coeff d q).coeff i = if i = 0 then coeff d f else 0

/-- **Main theorem, approximate form.** The same bound, for circuits that only approximate. -/
def MainTheoremApprox : Prop :=
  ∃ c : R, 0 < c ∧ ∀ p : N, ∃ m0 : N, ∀ (F : Type u) [Field F] [CharP F p], ∀ m ≥ m0 , ∀ s : N,
    (Approximates (hc F m) s → Beyond c m s) ∧ (p ̸= 2 → Approximates (perm F m) s → Beyond c m s)

/-! ### Valiant's classes -/

/-- A family of polynomials, with finitely many variables for every `n`. -/
structure Family (F : Type u) [CommSemiring F] where
  /-- the variables of the `n`-th polynomial -/
  vars : N → Type
  /-- finitely many -/
  [fin : ∀ n, Fintype (vars n)]
  /-- the `n`-th polynomial -/
  poly : ∀ n, MvPolynomial (vars n) F

attribute [instance] Family.fin

/-- VP: the number of variables, the degree and the circuit size are polynomially bounded in `n`. -/
def VP (F : Type u) [CommSemiring F] : Set (Family F) :=
  {f | ∃ a : N, ∀ n, Fintype.card (f.vars n) ≤ n ^ a + a ∧ (f.poly n).totalDegree ≤ n ^ a + a ∧
    Computes (f.poly n) (n ^ a + a)}

/-- The closure of VP: the same with approximating circuits. -/
def VPBar (F : Type u) [Field F] : Set (Family F) :=
  {f | ∃ a : N, ∀ n, Fintype.card (f.vars n) ≤ n ^ a + a ∧ (f.poly n).totalDegree ≤ n ^ a + a ∧
    Approximates (f.poly n) (n ^ a + a)}

/-- VNP: the families `f n =     g n (x, e)`, as polynomials, the sum over all vectors `e` of `r n` bits,
                               ∑︀
for some family `g` in VP. -/
def VNP (F : Type u) [CommSemiring F] : Set (Family F) :=
  {f | ∃ (r : N → N) (g : ∀ n, MvPolynomial (f.vars n ⊕ Fin (r n)) F),
    ({ vars := fun n =>∑︀
                        f.vars n ⊕ Fin (r n), poly := g } : Family F) ∈ VP F ∧
      ∀ n, f.poly n =      e : Fin (r n) → Bool, aeval (Sum.elim X fun i => if e i then 1 else 0) (g n)}

/-- **VP ̸= VNP**: over every field VP is a proper subset of VNP. -/
def Separation : Prop := ∀ (F : Type u) [Field F], VP F ⊂ VNP F

/-- Over every field VNP is not contained in the closure of VP. -/
def SeparationClosure : Prop := ∀ (F : Type u) [Field F], ¬ VNP F ⊆ VPBar F

end Statement

                                   Appendix: the full proof
   The appendices give the written proof of the results of Section 1, in the order of the
overview in Section 2. Appendices A to K prove the lower bound for the permanent over
the real numbers, and Appendix L assembles it. Appendix M passes to all fields and to the
Hamiltonian cycle polynomial, and Appendix N to approximate computation. Appendix O
proves the standard facts on CAT(0) spaces that are used, Appendix P lists the terms coined
here, and Appendix Q describes the Lean development. Appendices A to L are the sections
of the proof, and the text refers to them as “Section A” to “Section L”. Every numbered
statement of the appendices except Remark N.6 has a Lean theorem, whose name is formed
from the kind of the statement and a short name, such as thm_alt for Theorem D.2; Table 3
lists these names.
   The printed proofs differ in completeness. Those of Sections A to G and of Appendix O,
and the deduction of Theorem L.1 in Section L, are meant to be complete, if terse. The proofs
of Proposition I.1, Theorem H.2(d), (e), Theorem J.3 and Proposition N.4 describe machines
and programs and account for their sizes and running times. That these do exactly what is
claimed can be verified only in the Lean development, and each of these four statements is
followed by a formalization note that says so. Consequently the contracting case, and with
it every main result, rests on Lean at these points.
   Table 1 shows how the sections depend on each other. Section E treats the flat case
completely, and nothing after it is needed for that case except Section L. The proof of the
duality theorem in Section A can be skipped at a first reading, since the theorem is used
only in Section G. The standard facts on CAT(0) spaces that Section C collects are proved
in Appendix O. The main terms that are introduced in this paper or used in a special sense
are listed in Appendix P.
Notation and conventions. Recall that log is the logarithm to the base two. We write ln for
the natural logarithm, and for an integer 𝑛 ≥ 1 we let lg 𝑛 = ⌊log 𝑛⌋ + 1 be the number of its
binary digits. The set N contains 0, [𝑘] = {1, . . . , 𝑘}, and for a condition 𝑃 the bracket [𝑃 ]
is 1 if 𝑃 holds and 0 otherwise. The letters 𝑐 > 0 and 𝐶 denote a small and a large constant
whose values may differ from one statement to the next, and inside a proof a constant taken
from another statement carries the name of that statement as a subscript. Constants with
names of their own, such as 𝜅 (the constant of Lemma B.1), 𝜇, 𝐶0 , 𝐶1 , ℎ, 𝑏 and 𝐻, keep
their values once they are chosen, and from Section G on the constants in 𝑂(·) and the word
“large” depend on these choices only. The letters Λ, 𝑁 and 𝑠 are reused with local meanings,
each introduced where it is used. In Sections A and E the letter 𝑐 also denotes the factor by

        Section   what is proved                                       uses         case
        A         spectral points and prices; duality; random points   –            both
        B         the inequality (B.2) for two crossing wires          A            both
        C         prices satisfy the inequality of Bruhat and Tits     A, B         both
        D         the flat case or the contracting case holds          C            both
        E         lower bounds for 𝑓𝑘,ℓ and for the permanent          A, C, D      flat
        F         a Poincaré inequality on unitriangular groups        C, D         contracting
        G         rerouting laws                                       A, F         contracting
        H         every network is a sum of tree networks              G            contracting
        I         programs and tape automata; runs as networks         G, H         contracting
        J         computations can be cut into pieces                  H, I         contracting
        K         small circuits give accelerators; there are none     E, G, I, J   contracting
        L         Theorems L.1, L.2, Corollary L.3                     D, E, K      both
        M         all fields; the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial         D, E, K      both
        N         approximate computation                              E, K, M      both

and with a subscript, as in E𝑔 , it is the uniform average over a finite set.

                   Appendix A. Tensors, spectral points and prices
   In this section we compare tensors shared by finitely many parties by restriction, as in
Strassen’s theory of the asymptotic spectrum [Str87, Str88]. A spectral point measures such
tensors compatibly with restrictions, products and sums, it induces a pseudometric on the
parties, and the prices are the limits of multiples of these pseudometrics. Lemma A.3 is our
link to circuits, Theorem A.6 is used only in Section G, and Propositions A.7 and A.8 add
new points to a price. Section A.4 and the proofs in Sections A.5 and A.6 rest on Zorn’s
lemma and may be skipped at a first reading. Of the field of coefficients k = R we use only
that it is a field in which 2 ̸= 0, and 2 ̸= 0 is needed only for the permanent.

A.1. Tensors, restrictions and covers. Let 𝐼 be a finite nonempty set, whose elements
we call parties, as in quantum information theory. A tensor on 𝐼 is an array with entries in k
that has one index for each party, which runs through a finite set, the index set at this       ⨂︀
                                                                                                    party.
A tensor may be zero. Following Strassen, we say that 𝑠 is a restriction of 𝑡 if 𝑠 = ( 𝑝 𝐴𝑝 ) 𝑡
for some linear maps 𝐴𝑝 , one for each party. We think of the parties as players who share
the state 𝑡 and may each process their own index. We say that 𝑠 is covered by 𝑡1 , . . . , 𝑡𝑟 , and
that these form a cover of 𝑠, if 𝑠 = 𝑠1 + · · · + 𝑠𝑟 with every 𝑠𝑗 a restriction of 𝑡𝑗 , and we
measure a cover by the number 𝑟 of its tensors.
   In the product 𝑠𝑡 each party holds its index of 𝑠 and its index of 𝑡, and 1 denotes the tensor
all of whose index sets have one element and whose entry is 1. Tensors that are restrictions
of each other are identified, so that the product is commutative and associative, with unit 1.
Every nonzero tensor has 1 as a restriction, and every tensor is covered by finitely many
copies of 1.
   For a map 𝜋 : 𝐼 → 𝐽 of finite sets the push-forward 𝜋𝑡 of a tensor 𝑡 on 𝐼 is the tensor
on 𝐽 in which the party 𝜋(𝑝) holds the index of 𝑝, and a party outside the image holds an
index set with one element. When 𝐽 has two elements, 𝜋𝑡 is a matrix, a flattening of 𝑡.
Push-forward commutes with products and with the composition of maps, and it sends 1 to
1, restrictions to restrictions and covers to covers. In particular, a tensor on a subset 𝑃 ⊆ 𝐼
is also a tensor on 𝐼.
   For a nonempty 𝑃 ⊆ 𝐼 and an integer 𝑞 ≥ 1 the unit tensor of size 𝑞 on 𝑃 is ⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 =
∑︀𝑞 ⨂︀                                                              𝑞
   𝑖=1     𝑝∈𝑃 𝑒𝑖 , where 𝑒1 , . . . , 𝑒𝑞 is the standard basis of k . For parties 𝑢 ̸= 𝑣 we call ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑣 =
⟨𝑞⟩{𝑢,𝑣} the wire of dimension 𝑞 between 𝑢 and 𝑣, and we put ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑢 = 1. A product of
wires is a graph tensor. In the literature on graph tensors [CVZ19, CZ19, VC17] a wire is
an EPR pair of level 𝑞, and in tensor networks [Orús14] it is a bond of dimension 𝑞. Since
⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 ⟨𝑞 ′ ⟩𝑃 = ⟨𝑞𝑞 ′ ⟩𝑃 , a wire of dimension 2𝑛 is a product of 𝑛 wires of dimension two, and we
say that it carries 𝑛 bits.
Example. On two parties 𝑢, 𝑣 a tensor is a matrix, two matrices are restrictions of each
other if and only if they have the same rank, and the matrix of rank 𝑞 ≥ 1 is the wire ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑣 .
On three parties the restrictions of ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑣𝑤 are the tensors of tensor rank at most 𝑞, and
⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑣 ⟨𝑞⟩𝑣𝑤 ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑤 is the tensor of the multiplication of 𝑞 × 𝑞 matrices.
A.2. Spectral points, wires and hubs. We measure tensors by functions that are com-
patible with the three notions just introduced.
                                                 VP ̸= VNP                                       19

Definition A.1. A spectral point on 𝐼 is a function 𝜑 ≥ 0 on the tensors on 𝐼 ∑︀   such that
𝜑(1) = 1, such that 𝜑(𝑠𝑡) = 𝜑(𝑠)𝜑(𝑡) for all tensors 𝑠 and 𝑡, and such that 𝜑(𝑠) ≤ 𝑟𝑗=1 𝜑(𝑡𝑗 )
whenever 𝑠 is covered by 𝑡1 , . . . , 𝑡𝑟 .
    For 𝑟 = 1 the last condition says that 𝜑 is monotone under restriction, and for 𝑟 = 0
it says that 𝜑(0) = 0. Since 1 is a restriction of every nonzero tensor, 𝜑(𝑡) ≥ 1 for every
𝑡 ̸= 0. Examples are the function that is 1 on all nonzero tensors and the rank of a flattening.
This class is larger than Strassen’s asymptotic spectrum [Str88], whose points are in addition
additive under direct sums and normalized on unit tensors, and for example 𝜑𝑎 is again a
spectral point for 0 < 𝑎 ≤ 1.
    The next lemma describes the values of a spectral point on unit tensors, and in particular
on wires.
Lemma A.2 (exponents of unit tensors). Let 𝜑 be a spectral point on 𝐼. For every nonempty
𝑃 ⊆ 𝐼 there is a real number 𝑔𝜑 (𝑃 ) ≥ 0 such that 𝜑(⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 ) = 𝑞 𝑔𝜑 (𝑃 ) for all 𝑞 ≥ 1. The function
                                       𝑢 ̸= 𝑣 and by 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢, 𝑢) = 0 is a pseudometric on 𝐼 with
defined by 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑔𝜑 ({𝑢, 𝑣}) for ∑︀
values in [0, 1]. Moreover 𝑔𝜑 (𝑃 ) ≤ 𝑒 𝑑𝜑 (𝑒), where 𝑒 runs over the edges of any tree with
vertex set 𝑃 .
  We call 𝑔𝜑 (𝑃 ) the exponent of 𝑃 under 𝜑.
Proof. Each claim follows from a restriction between products of unit tensors. The function
𝑞 ↦→ 𝜑(⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 ) is at least 1, multiplicative, because ⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 ⟨𝑞 ′ ⟩𝑃 = ⟨𝑞𝑞 ′ ⟩𝑃 , and nondecreasing,
because ⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 is a restriction of ⟨𝑞 ′ ⟩𝑃 for 𝑞 ≤ 𝑞 ′ , and hence it is a power of 𝑞. A wire of
dimension 𝑞 is covered by 𝑞 copies of 1, so 𝑑𝜑 ≤ 1. Contracting the two indices at 𝑣 shows
that ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑤 is a restriction of ⟨𝑞⟩𝑢𝑣 ⟨𝑞⟩𝑣𝑤 , which gives the triangle inequality. Identifying the
indices at every vertex of a tree in the same way shows that ⟨𝑞⟩𝑃 is a restriction of the
product of the wires on the edges of the tree, which gives the last inequality.                □
  The next lemma routes a tensor of rank at most 𝑟 across a partition of the parties through
two new parties, which we call hubs, as in Figure 4.

A.3. Prices. By Lemma A.2 a spectral point 𝜑 gives the value 2𝑛 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢,𝑣) to a wire that
carries 𝑛 bits between 𝑢 and 𝑣, so 𝑑𝜑 (𝑢, 𝑣) is what 𝜑 charges for one bit of such a wire, which
explains the name in the next definition.
Definition A.4. A price on a finite set 𝑉 is a limit of functions 𝑐 𝑑𝜑 , where 𝑐 ≥ 0 is a real
number and 𝜑 is a spectral point on 𝑉 . A function on the pairs of elements of an arbitrary
set 𝑌 is a price if all its pull-backs to finite sets are prices. We write |𝑥𝑦| for the distance of
𝑥 and 𝑦 in a given price.
  Here limits are pointwise, and the pull-back of a function 𝑤 on the pairs of 𝑌 along a
map 𝑔 : 𝑉 → 𝑌 is (𝑥, 𝑦) ↦→ 𝑤(𝑔(𝑥), 𝑔(𝑦)). Prices are pseudometrics. Pull-backs, nonnegative
multiples and pointwise limits of prices are prices, where for a map 𝑔 : 𝑉 → 𝑊 of finite sets
and a spectral point 𝜑 on 𝑊 one uses the spectral point 𝑡 ↦→ 𝜑(𝑔𝑡) on 𝑉 . The prices that
are bounded by a given function form a compact set, by Tychonoff’s theorem.
A.4. Upper functionals. Spectral points are hard to construct directly, because they must
be exactly multiplicative, whereas submultiplicative functions arise easily as infima over
covers. The tensor rank is such a function, and for each of them we find a spectral point
that is nowhere larger (Proposition A.5).
   A function 𝑝 ≥ 0 on ∑︀the tensors on 𝐼 is an upper functional if 𝑝(1) = 1, 𝑝(𝑠𝑡) ≤ 𝑝(𝑠)𝑝(𝑡)
for all 𝑠, 𝑡, and 𝑝(𝑠) ≤ 𝑗 𝑝(𝑡𝑗 ) whenever 𝑠 is covered by 𝑡1 , . . . , 𝑡𝑟 . One function is below
another if it is at most as large at every tensor. For tensors 𝑥, 𝑦 and a real number 𝑐 ≥ 0 we
say that 𝑝 respects the triple (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑐) if 𝑝(𝑡𝑥) ≤ 𝑐 𝑝(𝑡𝑦) for every tensor 𝑡.
Proposition A.5 (spectral points below upper functionals). Below every upper functional
there is a spectral point that respects every triple which the upper functional respects.
Proof. We show in two steps that a minimal upper functional is multiplicative. The upper
functionals below the given one that respect the triples which it respects are closed under
infima of chains, so by Zorn’s lemma there is a minimal one, which we call 𝑝.
   Let 𝑟(𝑡) = inf 𝑛≥1 𝑝(𝑡𝑛 )1/𝑛 , which by Fekete’s lemma is also the limit for 𝑛 → ∞. Clearly
𝑟 ≤ 𝑝, 𝑟(1) = 1 and 𝑟(𝑠𝑡) ≤ 𝑟(𝑠)𝑟(𝑡). If 𝑠 is covered by 𝑡1 , . . . , 𝑡𝑙 , then 𝑠𝑛 is covered by their
words of length 𝑛. For 𝛿 > 0 let 𝑐𝛿 be a constant with 𝑝(𝑡𝑎𝑗 ) ≤ 𝑐𝛿 (𝑟(𝑡𝑗 ) + 𝛿)𝑎 for all 𝑗 and all
                                                                              𝑎

hence 𝑝(𝑡𝑓 ) = 𝑝(𝑡)𝑝(𝑓 ). If 𝑝(𝑓 ) = 0, then 𝑝(𝑡𝑓 ) = 0 as well, so 𝑝 is multiplicative.             □
A.5. Duality. The next theorem is a form of Strassen’s duality theorem [Str88], for the
larger class of spectral points of Definition A.1.
Theorem A.6 (duality). Let 𝐶 ̸= 0 and 𝐴1 , . . . , 𝐴𝑟 be tensors on 𝐼, not all 𝐴𝛽 zero, and
suppose that every spectral point 𝜑 on 𝐼 satisfies 𝜑(𝐶) ≤ max𝛽 𝜑(𝐴𝛽 ). Then for every 𝜀 > 0
and all large 𝑁 the power 𝐶 𝑁 is covered by at most 2𝜀𝑁 tensors, each of which is a product
of 𝑁 factors from 𝐴1 , . . . , 𝐴𝑟 .
   Informally, if no spectral point detects that 𝐶 is larger than all the 𝐴𝛽 , then 𝐶 𝑁 can be
made from products of 𝑁 of the 𝐴𝛽 with only 2𝜀𝑁 summands. All identities of Sections G
to J arise in this way, and none of them is written down explicitly. For background see
[Str87, BS83b, Zui19, Fri21, Vra22] and the survey [WZ26], and for an earlier use of duality
in circuit complexity see Robere and Zuiddam [RZ21].

   Certificates can be multiplied, and they can be raised to a common level, by multiplying

increases, so it stays at most 1, and each final term has fewer than 𝑘 factors 𝐶 and, having
𝑁 factors in all, weight at least 𝜂 𝑁 . Hence there are at most 𝜂 −𝑁 terms. Each remaining
factor 𝐶 is covered by a fixed number 𝑐 of copies of a nonzero 𝐴𝛽 , and replacing it in this
way gives at most 𝑐𝑘 𝜂 −𝑁 ≤ 2𝜀𝑁 terms for large 𝑁 .                                            □
A.6. Extension and random points. In Sections C and E we add new points to a price
by placing each of them at one of the old points, chosen at random, and Proposition A.8
below provides an enlarged price in which distances are at most the expected distances of
these positions. It follows from the next proposition for spectral points, in which 𝐼 is the
set of old parties, 𝐼 ′ is the enlarged set, the maps 𝜋𝑖 are the possible positions of the new
parties and the 𝜃𝑖 are their probabilities.

with 𝑝(𝜄𝐴) = 𝜑(𝐴) for all 𝐴.
    If 𝐶 = 0, Proposition A.5 finishes the proof. Otherwise let 𝑐 = 𝜑(𝐶) and 𝑞(𝑡) =
inf 𝑚≥0 𝑝(𝑡 𝜄𝐶 𝑚 )/𝑐𝑚 . As 𝑝(𝜄𝐶 𝑚 ) = 𝑐𝑚 , this is an upper functional below 𝑝 that respects
the triple (1, 𝜄𝐶, 1/𝑐), as in the last step of the proof of Proposition A.5. By Proposition A.5
there is a spectral point 𝜑′ below 𝑞 that respects this triple, and it satisfies 𝜑′ ≤ 𝑝 ≤ 𝑓 and
𝑐 ≤ 𝜑′ (𝜄𝐶) ≤ 𝑓 (𝜄𝐶) = 𝑐.                                                                            □
   We now state the consequence for prices.
Proposition A.8 (random points). Let 𝑤 be a price on 𝑌 , let 𝑄 be a set disjoint from
𝑌 , and let 𝜎 be a random map 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄 → 𝑌 that is the identity on 𝑌 , such that each 𝜎(𝑜)
has only finitely many values. Then there is a price on 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄 that extends 𝑤 and satisfies
|𝑜𝑜′ | ≤ E |𝜎(𝑜)𝜎(𝑜′ )| for all 𝑜, 𝑜′ ∈ 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄.
22                                           CLAUDE

Proof. We reduce to finite sets by compactness and to a price of the form 𝑑𝜑 by a limit, and
then apply Proposition A.7. It is enough to find, for all finite sets 𝑌0 ⊆ 𝑌 and 𝑄0 ⊆ 𝑄 such
that 𝜎 maps 𝑄0 into 𝑌0 , a price on 𝑌0 ∪ 𝑄0 with the two properties, because these prices
form compact sets, so that one price on 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄 then works for all of them at once. Now let
𝑌 and 𝑄 be finite, and let 𝜎 take the values 𝜋𝑖 with probabilities 𝜃𝑖 . Passing to a limit, we
may assume that 𝑤 = 𝑐 𝑑𝜑 , and then that 𝑐 = 1 by homogeneity. Let 𝐶 be the product of
all wires ⟨2⟩𝑢𝑣 with 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑌 , and apply Proposition A.7 to the inclusion of 𝑌 in 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄 and
the maps∑︀𝜋𝑖 . For every pair 𝑒 of elements of 𝑌 ∪ 𝑄 the first condition, applied to ⟨2⟩𝑒 , gives
𝑑𝜑′ (𝑒) ≤ 𝑖 𝜃𝑖 𝑑𝜑 (𝜋𝑖 𝑒), which is the expected distance. On 𝑌 this reads 𝑑𝜑′ ≤ 𝑑𝜑 , and by the
second condition the sums over all pairs in 𝑌 are equal, so equality holds for each pair. □

        Appendix B. An identity and an inequality for two crossing wires
   This section proves an identity and an inequality for four parties 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐, 𝑑 and two wires
that cross (Figure 2), one between 𝑎 and 𝑐 and one between 𝑏 and 𝑑. The crossing identity
(Proposition B.2) writes their product as a sum of tensors Ψ𝑁 , each of which is a sum of few
products with respect to both partitions {𝑎, 𝑑} | {𝑏, 𝑐} and {𝑎, 𝑏} | {𝑐, 𝑑}. By Lemma A.3
a spectral point turns this into the crossing inequality between distances in a price (Propo-
sition B.3), with its consequence (B.2). Only (B.2) is used later, and only in the proof of
Lemma C.2, so that the proof of the identity in Section B.3 may be skipped at first reading.
B.1. Frobenius symbols. A symbol is a pair (𝐼,      𝐽) of finite subsets of N with |𝐼| = |𝐽|.
The number |𝐼| is the size of the symbol, and 𝑖∈𝐼 (2𝑖 + 1) + 𝑗∈𝐽 (2𝑗 + 1) is its energy.

explains the name, but we use no property of partitions. For an integer 𝐸 ≥ 0 let 𝑃 (𝐸) be
the number

B.2. The crossing identity. We fix sizes 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ≥ 0 and energies 𝑒1 , 𝑒2 ≥ 0, and let Σ1 and

tensor is called integral if all its entries are integers. With this notation the crossing identity
reads as follows.

B.4. The crossing inequality. We now apply spectral points to the crossing identity. In
the resulting inequality, the points 𝑥, 𝑣, 𝑦, 𝑢 play the roles of the parties 𝑎, 𝑐, 𝑏, 𝑑, the points
𝑜, 𝑜′ and 𝑤, 𝑤′ are the hubs in the two products of wires on the right of Figure 2, and 𝑟, 𝑟′
measure the dimensions of the two dotted wires on a logarithmic scale.
Proposition B.3 (crossing inequality). Let 𝑥, 𝑣, 𝑦, 𝑢, 𝑜, 𝑜′ , 𝑤, 𝑤′ be points of a price and

of

                             𝑝|𝑥𝑤| + 𝑞|𝑢𝑤| + 𝑞|𝑦𝑤′ | + 𝑝|𝑣𝑤′ | + 𝑟′ |𝑤𝑤′ |.

Proof. We reduce the statement to the metric 𝑑𝜑 of a spectral point 𝜑, and apply 𝜑 to the

𝑑𝜑 for a spectral point 𝜑 on the eight points. For a large integer 𝑛0 let 𝐸1 = ⌊𝑝2 𝑛0 ⌋ and
𝐸2 = ⌊𝑞 2 𝑛0 ⌋. By the pigeonhole principle there is, for 𝑖 = 1, 2, a class Σ𝑖 as in Section B.2, of

some size 𝑛𝑖 and some energy 𝑒𝑖 ≤ 𝐸𝑖 , that contains at least the fraction (𝐸𝑖 + 1)−2 , and of

course at most all, of the symbols of energy at most 𝐸𝑖 . So, in units of 𝜅 𝑛0 , the logarithms
of |Σ1 | and |Σ2 | are 𝑝 + 𝑜(1) and 𝑞 + 𝑜(1) by Lemma B.1. We identify 𝑎, 𝑐, 𝑏, 𝑑 with 𝑥, 𝑣, 𝑦, 𝑢,
and write |𝑥𝑣| for 𝑑𝜑 (𝑥, 𝑣). The product of the two wires is covered by the Θ + 1 tensors Ψ𝑁

of Proposition B.2(a), the spectral point 𝜑 is subadditive on covers, and ln(Θ + 1) = 𝑜( 𝑛0 ).

Hence, by Lemma A.2, some 𝑁 has ln 𝜑(Ψ𝑁 ) ≥ (𝑝|𝑥𝑣| + 𝑞|𝑦𝑢| − 𝑜(1))𝜅 𝑛0 . Define 𝑟, 𝑟′ ≥ 0
by 𝑟2 = (Θ − 𝑁 )/𝑛0 and 𝑟′2 = 𝑁/𝑛0 , so that 𝑟2 + 𝑟′2 = Θ/𝑛0 ≤ 2(𝑝2 + 𝑞 2 ). In the same

at most 𝑟′ + 𝑜(1), by the upper bound of Lemma B.1. Lemma A.3, with the hubs 𝑜, 𝑜′ for (c)
and 𝑤, 𝑤′ for (b), bounds 𝜑(Ψ𝑁 ) in both ways. A limit of (𝑟, 𝑟′ ) along a subsequence of the
𝑛0 proves the first statement. Finally we add the two bounds and use 𝑟𝜆 ≤ 𝑡20 𝑟2 + 𝜆2 /(2𝑡0 ),
and the same with 𝑟′ , to obtain (B.2).                                                         □

                   Appendix C. Prices have nonpositive curvature
   In this section we show that every price satisfies the inequality of Bruhat and Tits that
characterizes nonpositive curvature (Theorem C.1), and that it is the pull-back of the metric
of a complete CAT(0) space whose metric is again a price (Proposition C.3). The only
properties of prices that we use are Proposition A.8, the inequality (B.2), and the fact that
pull-backs, nonnegative multiples and limits of prices are prices.

C.1. The curvature bound and the plan of its proof. The main result of this section
is the following.
Theorem C.1 (curvature bound for prices). Let 𝑥, 𝑣, 𝑚, 𝑧 be points of a price with
|𝑥𝑚|, |𝑣𝑚| ≤ 𝐷 and |𝑥𝑣| = 2𝐷. Then
(C.1)                           |𝑧𝑚|2 ≤ 21 |𝑧𝑥|2 + 12 |𝑧𝑣|2 − 𝐷2 .
   The hypotheses say that 𝑚 is a midpoint of 𝑥 and 𝑣 (in this section 𝑚 denotes a point, not
the size of a matrix). In the Euclidean plane (C.1) is the formula for the length of a median,
and in general it is the CN inequality of Bruhat and Tits [BT72], [BH99, Exercise II.1.9(1)(c)].
Throughout the proof we write Γ = |𝑚𝑧|2 − 12 |𝑥𝑧|2 − 12 |𝑣𝑧|2 , so that the claim is Γ ≤ −𝐷2 .
   The plan of the proof is as follows. If a price on Z2 is given by a seminorm 𝑁 , then (B.2)
forces the parallelogram law for 𝑁 , so that three values of 𝑁 determine all others. One
would like to differentiate the price at 𝑚, but the proof avoids differentiation. Instead, for a
large integer 𝑛, we use Proposition A.8 to join 𝑥 and 𝑣 to 𝑚 by paths of new points 𝛾𝑖 , and
every 𝛾𝑖 to 𝑧 by a path of new points 𝐺(𝑖, 𝑗) (Figure 5). This family of points is called a fan,
and it has two sheets, one for 𝑥 and one for 𝑣, because only the two paths to 𝑚 together have
a known length, namely 2𝐷. We multiply distances by 𝑛2 and average their squares over
boxes in the coordinates (𝑖, 𝑘), where 𝑗 = 𝑖 + 𝑘 + 𝑛. The boxes are short in 𝑘, so that 𝑗/𝑛2
follows 𝑖/𝑛2 . The inequality (B.2) survives this averaging, and the averages depend only on
the displacement and define a seminorm 𝑁 that satisfies the parallelogram law (Lemma C.2).
We bound 𝑁 in three directions, and in a fourth direction it contains Γ by a telescoping sum,
so that the parallelogram law compares them.

C.2. Averaged squared distances. We now describe the averaging in general. We consider
two disjoint copies of Z2 , the two sheets, so that 𝑃 + 𝑔 lies on the sheet of 𝑃 for 𝑔 ∈ Z2 , and
we write ‖𝑔‖1 = |𝑔1 | + |𝑔2 |. Suppose that for every 𝑛 = 1, 2, . . . we are given a price 𝑊𝑛 on
the union of the two sheets, and that there is a constant 𝐾 with 𝑊𝑛 (𝑃, 𝑃 + 𝑔) ≤ 𝐾‖𝑔‖1 for
all 𝑛, all points 𝑃 and all 𝑔 ∈ Z2 . The 𝑛th box consists of the points (𝑖, 𝑘) with 0 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑛2
and 0 ≤ 𝑘 < 𝑛, on both sheets. For a sequence 𝑓 = (𝑓𝑛 ) of uniformly bounded functions
on the two sheets, the average E𝑓 is the limit, along a fixed free ultrafilter on the indices
𝑛, of the mean of 𝑓𝑛 over the 𝑛th box. The average is linear, positive and invariant under

seminorm is Euclidean.

Since Λ2 ≤ (𝐷 + |𝑧𝑚|)2 , dividing by 𝜃 and letting 𝜃 → 0 gives Γ ≤ −𝐷2 , which is (C.1). □
C.4. Price spaces. Sections D and F use Theorem C.1 in another form: every price is the
pull-back of the metric of a complete space of nonpositive curvature, in which geodesics,
barycenters and nearest-point projections are available.
  A midpoint of two points 𝑥, 𝑣 of a metric space is a point at the distance 21 |𝑥𝑣| from both,
and it satisfies the CN inequality [BT72] if (C.1) holds for it with 𝐷 = 21 |𝑥𝑣| and with every
point 𝑧.
Definition. A metric space is CAT(0) if it is complete and if any two of its points have a
midpoint that satisfies the CN inequality. A price space is a CAT(0) space whose metric is
a price in the sense of Definition A.4.
   This is the definition used by Sturm [Stu03], and for complete geodesic spaces it is equiv-
alent to the more common one by comparison triangles [BH99, Exercise II.1.9(1)(c)]. The
midpoint in the definition is the only midpoint of the two points, as the CN inequality shows
when 𝑧 is another midpoint. The following proposition says that every price arises from a
price space.
Proposition C.3 (embedding into a price space). Every price on a set 𝑉 is the pull-back
of the metric of a price space.
Proof. We add midpoints to 𝑉 again and again and then pass to the completion, using
Proposition A.8 for the midpoints and Theorem C.1 for the CN inequality.
   Let 𝑉0 = 𝑉 , and let 𝑉𝑙+1 be 𝑉𝑙 with one new point for every pair of its points. A price
on 𝑉𝑙 extends to one on 𝑉𝑙+1 by Proposition A.8, applied to the random map that fixes 𝑉𝑙
and sends each new point to either point of its pair with probability 12 , and in it each new
point is a midpoint of its pair. These prices extend one another, so together they form a
price on the union of the 𝑉𝑙 , in which any two points have a midpoint and, by Theorem C.1,
every midpoint satisfies the CN inequality. We identify points at distance 0 and let 𝑌 be the
completion, so that the given price on 𝑉 is the pull-back of the metric of 𝑌 . This metric is a
price, as a limit of prices, so by Theorem C.1 every midpoint in 𝑌 satisfies the CN inequality.
Finally, let 𝑥, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑌 , choose points 𝑥𝑛 → 𝑥 and 𝑣𝑛 → 𝑣 in the union of the 𝑉𝑙 , and let 𝑚𝑛 be
a midpoint of 𝑥𝑛 and 𝑣𝑛 . The CN inequality for 𝑚𝑛 at 𝑧 = 𝑚𝑘 shows that (𝑚𝑛 ) is a Cauchy
sequence, and its limit is a midpoint of 𝑥 and 𝑣.                                            □
C.5. Standard facts on CAT(0) spaces. The next lemma collects the standard facts
about CAT(0) spaces that we use [BH99, Stu03, BN08], and Appendix O gives their short
proofs from the CN inequality. Part (b) is Reshetnyak’s comparison for quadrilaterals [Res68,
KS93], whose weak form is Enflo’s roundness 2 [Enf69, Sat09], part (c) is in [Stu03], part (d)
is in [BH99, II.2], and part (f) is close to the Flat Quadrilateral Theorem [BH99, II.2.11];
our proof of it uses the inequality of part (b) in the form of the quadrilateral cosine of Berg
and Nikolaev [BN08]. Section D uses parts (a), (b) and (f), and Section F uses parts (a), (c),
(d) and (e).
    We call a subset of a CAT(0) space convex if it contains the midpoints of its points. The
𝐿 product 𝑌 𝑛 in part (e) carries the metric

Lemma C.4 (standard facts on CAT(0) spaces). Let 𝑌 be a CAT(0) space.
 (a) For 𝑥, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑌 and 𝑡 ∈ [0, 1] exactly one point 𝛾(𝑡) has the distances 𝑡|𝑥𝑣| from 𝑥 and
      (1 − 𝑡)|𝑥𝑣| from 𝑣, and |𝑧𝛾(𝑡)|2 ≤ (1 − 𝑡)|𝑧𝑥|2 + 𝑡|𝑧𝑣|2 − 𝑡(1 − 𝑡)|𝑥𝑣|2 for every 𝑧 ∈ 𝑌 .
      We write [𝑥, 𝑣] for the set of these points, the geodesic segment from 𝑥 to 𝑣, and we call
      𝛾(𝑡) the point at 𝑡 on [𝑥, 𝑣].
 (b) For 𝑥, 𝑣, 𝑦, 𝑢 ∈ 𝑌 we have |𝑥𝑣|2 + |𝑦𝑢|2 ≤ |𝑥𝑢|2 + |𝑦𝑣|2 + 2|𝑥𝑦||𝑢𝑣|. In particular the
      squared diagonals of the quadrilateral with the consecutive vertices 𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑣, 𝑢 add up to
      at most its squared sides, that is, |𝑥𝑣|2 + |𝑦𝑢|2 ≤ |𝑥𝑦|2 + |𝑦𝑣|2 + |𝑣𝑢|2 + |𝑢𝑥|2 , and if
      there is equality, then opposite sides are equal.

                                      Appendix D. The two cases
   In this section we formulate two hypotheses on the field k, a flat one and a contracting
one, and we show that at least one of them holds (Theorem D.2). Section E proves the lower
bound for the permanent under the first hypothesis, and Sections F to K prove it under the
second.
D.1. The two hypotheses. Let Mat𝜇 (F2 ) be the additive group of the 𝜇 × 𝜇 matrices over
the field with two elements. For a price on it and 𝑟 ≤ 𝜇 let 𝐷(𝑟) ≥ 0 be the root mean
square distance of two matrices whose difference has rank 𝑟, that is, let 𝐷(𝑟)2 be the mean
of |𝑎, 𝑎 + 𝑊 |2 over all matrices 𝑎 and all matrices 𝑊 of rank 𝑟. We call matrices 𝑊1 , 𝑊2 , . . .
transversal if their column spaces are linearly independent and their row spaces are as well.
The rank√of their sum is then the sum of their ranks. Every price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) satisfies
𝐷(𝑟) ≤ 𝑟 𝐷(1), by averaging over the quadrilaterals of Figure 6 (Step 1 of the proof of
Theorem D.2).

30                                              CLAUDE

Definition D.1. We consider two hypotheses on the field k, on which the notion of a price
depends.
Flat hypothesis: All finite subsets of all Euclidean spaces, with their metrics, are prices.
Contracting hypothesis: There are an integer 𝜇 ≥ 2 and a real number 𝑠 with 1 ≤ 𝑠 and
      𝑠2 < 𝜇 such that 𝐷(𝜇) ≤ 𝑠 𝐷(1) for all prices on Mat𝜇 (F2 ).
We say that we are in the flat case if the flat hypothesis holds, and in the contracting case
if the contracting hypothesis holds.
                                                                             √
   Thus the contracting hypothesis says that for some 𝜇 the bound 𝐷(𝜇) ≤ 𝜇 𝐷(1) can be
improved by a constant factor. The main result of this section relates the two hypotheses.
Theorem D.2 (the two cases). At least one of the two hypotheses of Definition D.1 holds,
that is, one of the two cases occurs.
D.2. Proof of Theorem D.2. We assume that the contracting hypothesis fails and derive
the flat one. Steps 1 to 3 produce a price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) with equality in Lemma C.4(b) for
all quadrilaterals as in Figure 6, Steps 4 to 7 deduce that its squared distances are the ranks
of the differences, and Step 8 fills a Euclidean cube by Lemma C.4(f).
Proof. Suppose that the contracting hypothesis fails, and fix 𝜇 ≥ 3. By Proposition C.3
every price is pulled back from the metric of a CAT(0) space, so that Lemma C.4 applies to
the points of a price.
Step 1: subadditivity. Let (𝑊, 𝑊 ′ ) be a uniformly random transversal pair of matrices of
ranks 𝑖 and 𝑗, where 𝑖 + 𝑗 ≤ 𝜇, and let 𝑎 be uniformly random and independent of it. Then
𝑊 , 𝑊 ′ and 𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ are uniformly distributed among the matrices of ranks 𝑖, 𝑗 and 𝑖 + 𝑗.
Both diagonals of the quadrilateral 𝑎, 𝑎 + 𝑊 , 𝑎 + 𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ , 𝑎 + 𝑊 ′ have the difference
𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ , so the mean of the second inequality of Lemma C.4(b) over these quadrilaterals is
𝐷(𝑖 + 𝑗)2 ≤ 𝐷(𝑖)2 + 𝐷(𝑗)2 .
                                                                                            √
Step 2: a price with equality. As the contracting hypothesis fails, for every 𝑠 with 1 ≤ 𝑠 < 𝜇
there is a price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) with 𝐷(𝜇) > 𝑠 𝐷(1). Every distance in a price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ) is
at most a constant, which depends on 𝜇 only, times 𝐷(1), so we may scale these prices to
                                                            √
𝐷(1) = 1 and, by compactness, pass to a limit for 𝑠 → 𝜇, which is a price with 𝐷(1) = 1
and 𝐷(𝜇)2 ≥ 𝜇. By Step 1 it has 𝐷(𝑟)2 ≤ 𝑟 and 𝜇 ≤ 𝐷(𝜇)2 ≤ 𝐷(𝑟)2 + 𝐷(𝜇 − 𝑟)2 ≤ 𝜇, and
therefore 𝐷(𝑟)2 = 𝑟 for all 𝑟 ≤ 𝜇.
Step 3: equality for all quadrilaterals. In this price the inequality of Step 1, a mean of
finitely many inequalities, is an equality, so for every 𝑎 and every transversal pair (𝑊, 𝑊 ′ )
the quadrilateral of Step 1 has equality in the second statement of Lemma C.4(b). Put
𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 ) = |𝑎, 𝑎 + 𝑊 |2 . For transversal 𝑊, 𝑊 ′ opposite sides are equal by the lemma, and the
equality then expresses one diagonal by the other and the sides, that is,
(D.1)                     𝑛𝑎+𝑊 ′ (𝑊 ) = 𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 ),
(D.2)              |𝑎 + 𝑊, 𝑎 + 𝑊 ′ |2 = 2𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 ) + 2𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 ′ ) − 𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ ).

in 𝑊 and 𝑊 ′ . We claim that for transversal 𝑊1 , 𝑊2 , 𝑊3
