Then 𝑊 and the two matrices on the right are transversal as well, and (D.3) gives, for
𝜒(𝑢, 𝑥) = 𝐵𝑎 (𝑊, 𝑢𝑥T ),
                   𝜒(𝑢1 , 𝑥1 ) − 𝜒(𝑢1 , 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 ) = 𝜒(𝑢1 + 𝑢2 , 𝑥2 ) − 𝜒(𝑢2 , 𝑥2 ).
The right-hand side does not depend on 𝑥1 , and the left-hand side changes sign when 𝑥1
becomes 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 , which leaves the three matrices transversal, so both sides vanish. Thus 𝑥
may be changed by any vector outside its span with the row space of 𝑊 , and likewise 𝑢. As
𝜇 ≥ 3, for 𝑊 of rank one such moves connect all vectors outside its row space, respectively
column space, and any two matrices of rank one are both transversal to a third. As 𝐵𝑎 is
symmetric, it takes one value 𝜆(𝑎) on all transversal pairs of matrices of rank one.
Step 6: 𝜆 = 0. For transversal 𝑉, 𝑊 ′ we have 𝑛𝑎+𝑉 (𝑉 ) = 𝑛𝑎 (𝑉 ), we have 𝑛𝑎+𝑉 (𝑊 ′ ) =
𝑛𝑎 (𝑊 ′ ) by (D.1), and 𝑛𝑎+𝑉 (𝑉 + 𝑊 ′ ) = |𝑎 + 𝑉, 𝑎 + 𝑊 ′ |2 is given by (D.2), so that
𝐵𝑎+𝑉 (𝑉, 𝑊 ′ ) = −𝐵𝑎 (𝑉, 𝑊 ′ ). If 𝑉 and 𝑊 ′ have rank one, this says that 𝜆(𝑎 + 𝑉 ) = −𝜆(𝑎).
If 𝑉 has rank two and 𝑊 ′ has rank one, the two sides are 2𝜆(𝑎 + 𝑉 ) and −2𝜆(𝑎) by (D.3),
and again 𝜆(𝑎 + 𝑉 ) = −𝜆(𝑎). But a matrix 𝑉 of rank two is reached by two steps of rank
one, which give 𝜆(𝑎 + 𝑉 ) = 𝜆(𝑎). Hence 𝜆 = 0.
Step 7: the squared distance is the rank. By Step 6 and (D.3), 𝐵𝑎 vanishes on all transversal
pairs, that is, 𝑛𝑎 is additive on transversal sums. The exchange of Step 5, with 𝑊 = 0
and 𝑛𝑎 in place of 𝐵𝑎 , shows that 𝑛𝑎 has one value on all matrices of rank one. By (D.1)
this value does not depend on 𝑎, so it equals 𝐷(1)2 = 1. Hence |𝑎, 𝑎′ |2 = rk(𝑎 − 𝑎′ ) for all
𝑎, 𝑎′ ∈ Mat𝜇 (F2 ).
Step 8: a solid cube. By Step 7 the diagonal matrices with entries 0 and 1 form the vertices
of a Euclidean cube [0, 1]𝜇 in a price space. We fill it one coordinate at a time, sending
each segment in the new direction, whose ends are already mapped, to the geodesic segment
between their images. Lemma C.4(f), applied with ℎ = 1 to two such segments, shows that
the map stays isometric, and thus the metric of [0, 1]𝜇 is a price. Every finite subset of a
Euclidean space of dimension at most 𝜇 is, up to translation and scale, a subset of this cube,
and hence its metric is a price. As 𝜇 ≥ 3 was arbitrary, the flat hypothesis holds.         □
D.3. Remarks on the two cases. We do not show that the two cases exclude each other.
Outside
√︀        the contracting case not every price is Euclidean, because for 𝜇 ≥ 3 the price
   rk(𝑎 − 𝑎′ ) of the proof above is not. Theorem D.2 is not effective: we do not know which
case occurs, and the contracting case comes without a value for 𝜇.

                                Appendix E. The flat case
   In this section we prove Theorem E.11, the lower bound of Theorem L.1 for the permanent
in the flat case of Definition D.1. As in Section 2, we treat the polynomial 𝑓𝑘,ℓ of the complete
network, which is a permanent of size about 2𝑘ℓ , and write it by Lemmas E.4 and A.3 as
a sum of networks through two hubs. Only the statements that say so need the flat case.
Section E.3 proves a first bound with an unspecified threshold on ℓ, which shows the method,
and Sections E.4 to E.6 make the threshold explicit.
E.1. Sums over bits as permanents. The next proposition replaces Valiant’s complete-
ness theorem [Val79], for which [Bür00] and [Bür24, Section 2.7 of the arXiv version] give
the classical constructions.
Proposition E.1 (sums over bits as permanents). Let 𝑅 be a commutative ring with an
element 12 , let 𝑣 : 𝐼 → 𝑉 and 𝑤 : 𝐼 → 𝐾 be surjections of finite sets, and for every 𝑛 ∈ 𝐼 let
𝑊𝑛 be a 2 × 2 matrix over 𝑅 with entries 𝑊𝑛 (𝑥, 𝑟), 𝑥, 𝑟 ∈ {0, 1}. Then
                                ∑︁        ∑︁      ∏︁
                                                       𝑊𝑛 (𝑥𝑣(𝑛) , 𝑟𝑤(𝑛) )
                             𝑥∈{0,1}𝑉 𝑟∈{0,1}𝐾 𝑛∈𝐼
32                                               CLAUDE

is the permanent of an 𝑀 × 𝑀 matrix, for every 𝑀 ≥ 3|𝐼|, whose entries are 0, ±1, ± 12 and
sums of values 𝑊𝑛 with the same 𝑤(𝑛).
Proof. We read each row of a matrix as a linear form in variables that stand for the columns,
so that the permanent is the coefficient of the product of all these variables in the product
of all rows. Let 𝜎 and 𝜌 be permutations of 𝐼 whose cycles are the fibres of 𝑣 and of 𝑤,
respectively. For every 𝑛 ∈ 𝐼 the matrix has three columns, with variables 𝐴𝑛 , 𝐵𝑛 , 𝐶𝑛 , and
three rows, namely
                      1
                      2 (𝐴𝑛 + 𝐵𝑛 + 𝐴𝜎𝑛 − 𝐶𝑛 ),              𝐴𝑛 + 𝐵𝑛 − 𝐴𝜎𝑛 + 𝐶𝑛 ,
                   𝑊𝑛 (1, 1)𝐶𝑛 + 𝑊𝑛 (0, 1)𝐵𝑛 + 𝑊𝜌𝑛 (1, 0)𝐶𝜌𝑛 + 𝑊𝜌𝑛 (0, 0)𝐵𝜌𝑛 .
The squarefree part of the product of the first two is 𝐴𝑛 𝐵𝑛 + 𝐴𝜎𝑛 𝐶𝑛 , and we call the choice
of the first monomial 𝑥 = 1 at 𝑛 and that of the second 𝑥 = 0 at 𝑛. Every variable 𝐴𝑚 is
used exactly once if and only if 𝑥 is constant on every cycle of 𝜎, and then one variable is
left at every 𝑛, namely 𝐶𝑛 if 𝑥 = 1 and 𝐵𝑛 if 𝑥 = 0. We say that 𝑟 = 1 at 𝑛 if the third
row at 𝑛 takes one of its first two terms, and 𝑟 = 0 otherwise. Every variable that was left
is used exactly once
                   ∏︀
                      if and only if 𝑟 is constant on every cycle of 𝜌, and then the product of
the coefficients is 𝑛 𝑊𝑛 (𝑥, 𝑟). A diagonal of ones pads the matrix to any larger size.      □
     We apply the proposition to sums of the following kind.
Definition (clause sums). Let 𝑉 be a finite set, whose elements we call bits. A literal is a
condition of the form 𝑥𝑣 = 1 or 𝑥𝑣 = 0 on a single bit. A clause consists of a nonempty list
of literals and a weight. Let 𝐾 be a finite set of clauses such that every bit occurs in at least
one literal, and let 𝑤𝑗 be the weight of the clause 𝑗 ∈ 𝐾. The clause sum is
                                 {︃
                    ∑︁      ∏︁        𝑤𝑗   if 𝑥 satisfies all literals of the clause 𝑗,
                  𝑥∈{0,1}𝑉 𝑗∈𝐾
                                      1    otherwise.
Its number of literals is the total length of the lists of all clauses.
   Clauses of weight 1 may be added, so that the condition that every bit occurs in a literal
is no restriction.
Corollary E.2 (clause sums as permanents). Let 2 ̸= 0 in k. A clause sum with 𝑞 literals
in all, whose weights are constants or variables, is the permanent of an 𝑀 × 𝑀 matrix, for
every 𝑀 ≥ 3𝑞, with entries that are affine in one variable each.
Proof. We sum over a bit 𝑟𝑗 for every clause 𝑗, in which 𝑟𝑗 = 0 contributes 1 and 𝑟𝑗 = 1
contributes 𝑤𝑗 − 1 times the indicators of the literals of the clause, and we put the factor
𝑤𝑗 − 1 on the first literal. Then we apply Proposition E.1 with 𝐼 the set of all literals, with
the map 𝐼 → 𝑉 that sends a literal to its bit, and with the map 𝐼 → 𝐾 that sends it to its
clause. A sum of values 𝑊𝑛 with the same clause 𝑗 is then affine in 𝑤𝑗 .                     □
E.2. The network polynomial. Let 𝑘 ≥ 2 and ℓ ≥ 1, and write [𝑘] = {1, . . . , 𝑘}. The
complete network on 𝑘 nodes with ℓ bits on every edge is the complete graph on [𝑘] with
a string 𝜎(𝑎𝑏) ∈ {0, 1}ℓ on every edge 𝑎𝑏. Let 𝜎 run over all such assignments, and let
𝜎𝑎 = (𝜎(𝑎𝑏))𝑏̸=𝑎 collect the strings on the edges at the node 𝑎. With a variable 𝑥𝑎,𝜌 for every
node 𝑎 and every 𝜌 ∈ {0, 1}(𝑘−1)ℓ , the network polynomial is

the 𝑘 blocks, or set-multilinear in the usual terminology.

   The coefficients of 𝑓𝑘,ℓ form a tensor on the 𝑘 nodes, in which the node 𝑎 has the index

Lemma E.3 (the network polynomial as a permanent). Let 2 ̸= 0 in k, and let 𝑘 ≥ 2 and
ℓ ≥ 1. If PERM𝑀 has a circuit over k with 𝐿 gates and 𝑀 ≥ 3𝑘 3 ℓ 2𝑘ℓ , then 𝑓𝑘,ℓ has one
with 5𝐿 gates.
Proof. The polynomial 𝑓𝑘,ℓ is the clause sum over the bits of 𝜎 in which, for every node 𝑎
and every 𝜌, there is a clause with the weight 𝑥𝑎,𝜌 whose literals say that 𝜎𝑎 = 𝜌. It has
𝑘(𝑘 − 1)ℓ 2(𝑘−1)ℓ ≤ 𝑀/3 literals, so by Corollary E.2 it is the permanent of an 𝑀 × 𝑀 matrix
whose entries are affine in one variable each. We substitute these entries for the variables of
the given circuit, and an affine function of one variable takes five gates.                  □
   The next lemma concerns arbitrary circuits, and its proof is the argument behind depth
reduction [Hya79, VSBR83]. We call a set 𝑆 ⊆ [𝑘], or the set of blocks with numbers in 𝑆,
balanced if 𝑘 ≤ 3|𝑆| ≤ 2𝑘.

            Appendix F. A Poincaré inequality on unitriangular groups
   In this section and in Sections G to K we are in the contracting case, and we fix 𝜇 and
𝑠 as in Definition D.1. Let 𝑈𝑛 be the group of the upper unitriangular 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrices
over F2 . We number the coordinates by 0, . . . , 𝑛 − 1 and use the generators 𝑥𝑗 = 1 + 𝐸𝑗,𝑗+1 ,
0 ≤ 𝑗 < 𝑛−1, where 𝐸𝑗𝑘 is the matrix whose only nonzero entry is a 1 in row 𝑗 and column 𝑘.
The generators 𝑥𝑖 and 𝑥𝑗 commute if |𝑖 − 𝑗| ≥ 2.
   Only Theorem F.6 is used later. It bounds the mean squared distance of two random
elements in a price on 𝑈ℎ by 𝐶0 times the sum, over the generators, of the mean squared
distance of two neighbours in the Cayley graph, where 𝐶0 does not depend on ℎ. Section F.1
gives a weaker bound for each 𝑈𝑛 separately. In Sections F.2 and F.3 we let 𝑈ℎ act on a
CAT(0) space, we show that the sets of points fixed by two overlapping subgroups ∼      = 𝑈2𝑢 ,
called windows, meet almost at a right angle, and we reach a common fixed point by averaging
projections.
38                                             CLAUDE

F.1. Contracting length functions. A length function on a group is a function 𝑔 ↦→ |𝑔| ≥
0 with |1| = 0, |𝑔 −1 | = |𝑔| and |𝑔𝑔 ′ | ≤ |𝑔| + |𝑔 ′ |. We say that it contracts if for every
homomorphism 𝜃 from the additive group Mat𝜇 (F2 ) into the group, the mean of |𝜃𝑊 |2 over
the matrices 𝑊 of rank 𝜇 is at most 𝑠2 times the mean over the matrices of rank 1.
Remark (length functions from maps into price spaces). Let 𝑓 be a map of a finite group
                                                   )︀1/2
𝐺 into a price space. Then |𝑔| = E𝑎 |𝑓 (𝑎)𝑓 (𝑔𝑎)|2
                                 (︀
                                                         , with 𝑎 uniformly distributed in 𝐺, is
a length function on 𝐺 that contracts. Indeed, for a homomorphism 𝜃 : Mat𝜇 (F2 ) → 𝐺 and
𝑎 ∈ 𝐺, the pull-back of the metric along 𝑊 ↦→ 𝑓 (𝜃(𝑊 )𝑎) is a price on Mat𝜇 (F2 ), so it has
𝐷(𝜇)2 ≤ 𝑠2 𝐷(1)2 by the contracting hypothesis, and the mean over 𝑎 is the claim.
  The next lemma extends the contraction from the rank 𝜇 to all ranks, with the letter 𝐷
used as in Section D.
Lemma F.1 (growth of the mean length with the rank). Let 1 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑘, let 𝜃 be a ho-
momorphism from the additive group Mat𝑘×𝑘 (F2 ) into a group with a contracting length
function, and let 𝐷(𝑟) be the root mean square of |𝜃𝑊 | over the matrices 𝑊 of rank 𝑟. Then
𝐷(𝑟) ≤ 𝜇(𝑗 + 1)𝑠𝑗 𝐷(1), where 𝑗 = ⌊log𝜇 𝑟⌋.

and then we write 𝑟 in base 𝜇. For the first claim we compose 𝜃 with 𝑍 ↦→ 𝛼(𝑍 ⊗ 1𝑖 )𝛼′ ,
where 1𝑖 is the identity matrix of size 𝑖 and 𝛼 and 𝛼′ are uniformly random 𝑘 × 𝜇𝑖 and
𝜇𝑖 × 𝑘 matrices of full rank. This homomorphism from Mat𝜇 (F2 ) multiplies ranks by 𝑖, and
the image of a fixed 𝑍 is uniformly distributed among the matrices of its rank, so the mean
of the contraction inequality over 𝛼, 𝛼′ is 𝐷(𝜇𝑖)2 ≤ 𝑠2 𝐷(𝑖)2 . The second claim follows by
Minkowski’s inequality from |𝜃(𝑊 + 𝑊 ′ )| ≤ |𝜃𝑊 | + |𝜃𝑊 ′ | for a random    transversal pair
(𝑊, 𝑊 ′ ) as in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem D.2. Finally, if 𝑟 = 𝑡≤𝑗 𝑑𝑡 𝜇𝑡 with digits

0 ≤ 𝑑𝑡 < 𝜇, then the two claims give 𝐷(𝑟) ≤ 𝑡 𝑑𝑡 𝑠𝑡 𝐷(1) ≤ 𝜇(𝑗 + 1)𝑠𝑗 𝐷(1), because

F.2. Windows and their fixed points. Let 𝑈ℎ act by isometries on a CAT(0) space 𝑍,

F.4. Related work. For real-valued functions the inequality of Theorem F.6 is a spectral
gap for a walk on unitriangular matrices, as in Stong’s eigenvalue bounds [Sto95]; for the
mixing time of this walk see [PS13, NS23]. Almost right angles between commuting or
overlapping subgroups are used in a similar way in proofs of property (T) [DJ02, EJ10, Kas11,
Opp17]. Poincaré constants for nonlinear targets, and for CAT(0) targets in particular, are
studied in [Wan00, IN05, Gro03, MN13].

                                      Appendix G. Rerouting laws
   We are still in the contracting case. In this section the duality theorem (Theorem A.6)
turns the Poincaré inequality of Theorem F.6 into identities between tensors, which we call
rerouting laws, or simply laws (Definition G.1). A law writes a product of wires between
arbitrary pairs of elements of 𝑈ℎ as a sum of tensor networks whose wires run along edges
of the Cayley graph. Theorem G.2 provides finitely many laws whose size is controlled by
a constant 𝐶1 that does not depend on ℎ. That their coefficients lie in a finitely generated
ring matters only in Section K.

G.1. Edges, loads and the choice of ℎ. Let 𝑏 = |𝑈ℎ | = 2ℎ(ℎ−1)/2 . By an edge we
always mean an edge {𝑔, 𝑥𝑗 𝑔} of the Cayley graph of 𝑈ℎ for the generators 𝑥𝑗 = 1 + 𝐸𝑗,𝑗+1 ,
0 ≤ 𝑗 < ℎ − 1, and there are 𝑏/2 edges for each 𝑗. A load is a vector 𝑛 = (𝑛𝑒 ) of natural
numbers, indexed by all pairs 𝑒 of distinct elements of 𝑈ℎ or by the edges only, and we
measure loads by the norms

G.2. Laws. Informally, a law is a recipe for replacing wires between arbitrary pairs of ele-
ments of 𝑈ℎ , the slots, by wires along edges, which in this role are called channels. Figure 10
shows the simplest case, a single slot that is rerouted along a path. A law does the same for
a whole family of slots at once, and it may use a sum of several networks, numbered by an
index 𝑖.

                               slot: one bit, with a copy at each end


                                     𝑔1                         𝑔3
                        𝑔=𝑔0                     𝑔2
                                                                              𝑔 ′ =𝑔4

        Figure 10. A single slot (dashed) rerouted along a path of four channels,
        each of which carries one bit. Every 𝑔𝑘 tests that the bits that it sees agree,
        and the product of these tests, summed over the bits on the channels, is the
        wire ⟨2⟩𝑔𝑔′ .


Definition G.1 (law). Let 𝐴 be a commutative ring. A law on 𝑈ℎ over 𝐴 consists of the
following data.
    ∙ Slots. A finite family of pairs of distinct elements of 𝑈ℎ , the slots. They are counted by
      a load 𝜎 on pairs: 𝜎𝑒 is the number of slots equal to 𝑒. Every slot carries one bit, and
      each of its two ends has its own copy of this bit.
    ∙ Indices. A finite nonempty set ℐ of indices, with an injective code that assigns to every
      index a string of 𝜆 bits, where 𝜆 ≥ 1.
    ∙ Channels. For every 𝑖 ∈ ℐ a load len𝑖 on edges. In this role an edge 𝑒 is called a channel,
      and it carries len𝑖,𝑒 bits.
    ∙ Node tensors. For every 𝑖 ∈ ℐ and every element 𝑔 of 𝑈ℎ a function with values in 𝐴,
      the node tensor of (𝑖, 𝑔). Its arguments are the bits at 𝑔 of the slots that contain 𝑔, and
      the bits of the channels that contain 𝑔.
These data are required to satisfy, for all values of the bits at the ends of the slots,
           ∏︁                                             ∑︁ ∑︁ ∏︁
(G.2)           [the 𝜎𝑒 bits agree at both ends of 𝑒] =                      (node tensor of (𝑖, 𝑔)),
            𝑒                                             𝑖∈ℐ        𝑦   𝑔

where 𝑒 runs over all pairs, 𝑦 over the assignments of len𝑖,𝑒 bits to every channel 𝑒, and 𝑔
over 𝑈ℎ .
                                            VP ̸= VNP                                          43

Convention. When a law is applied, every element has to know the index 𝑖, so we let every
channel begin with the 𝜆 bits of the code of 𝑖, and give the node tensor 0 to an element whose
channels do not all begin with the same code, or begin with a string that is no code. Since
the edges connect 𝑈ℎ , codes that agree along all channels agree everywhere. The channel 𝑒
now carries 𝜆 + len𝑖,𝑒 bits, and this is the load that Theorem G.2(b) bounds.

              Appendix H. Every network is a sum of tree networks
   In this section we apply the laws of Theorem G.2 again and again, along a tower of finite
sets, to an arbitrary tensor network. By Theorem H.2, the value of a network on 𝑀 nodes
is a sum of values of networks whose nodes form a tree of small depth, in which the bound
44                                            CLAUDE

for the packets at any one node is smaller by a factor 𝑀 𝛾 than the bound for all packets
together. In Sections I and J the network will be a computation, and the tree will show
where to cut it. Here 𝛾 = 1/ log 𝑏.
H.1. A tower of finite groups. To apply the laws repeatedly we use groups with ℎ types
of edges, arranged in a cycle, such that leaving out any one type leaves copies of the Cayley
graph of 𝑈ℎ . Let
                            𝑅𝑛 = F2 [𝑠]/(𝑠𝑛+1 ),   𝐺𝑛 = SLℎ (𝑅𝑛 ),
number the coordinates by Z/ℎ, and put 𝜍 = 1 + 𝑠, 𝑋𝑘 = 1 + 𝐸𝑘−1,𝑘 for 𝑘 ̸= 0 and
𝑋0 = 1 + 𝜍𝐸ℎ−1,0 , where 𝐸𝑗𝑘 is a matrix unit. The letters 𝑠 and 𝜍 have this meaning in the
present subsection only. Let 𝐾𝑛 be the kernel of the reduction 𝑠 ↦→ 0 from 𝐺𝑛 to SLℎ (F2 ), a
                    2
2-group of order 2(ℎ −1)𝑛 .
Proposition H.1 (tower). Let 𝑛 ≥ 1 and 𝑀 = |𝐺𝑛 |. There are 𝐺𝑛 -sets 𝑉0 = 𝐺𝑛 , 𝑉1 , . . . , 𝑉𝐽 ,
with equivariant projections 𝜋 : 𝑉𝑗 → 𝑉𝑗+1 that are 𝑏 to one, such that
 (a) for every 𝑖 ∈ Z/ℎ the 𝑋𝑘 , 𝑘 ̸= 𝑖, generate a copy of 𝑈ℎ (with 𝑋𝑖+1 , . . . , 𝑋𝑖−1 as the
     generators in order), which acts freely on every 𝑉𝑗 ; we call its orbits cosets of type 𝑖;
 (b) 𝑐 and 𝑋𝑖 𝑐 always lie in different cosets of type 𝑖, and 𝜋 maps every coset of type 𝑖
     bijectively onto one, exactly 𝑏 of them onto each;
                                           2
 (c) |𝑉𝐽 | < 𝑏 |SLℎ (F2 )| and |𝐺𝑛+1 | = 2ℎ −1 |𝐺𝑛 |; thus |𝑉𝐽 | ≤ const, and 𝑀 runs through a
     sequence with bounded quotients as 𝑛 varies;
 (d) every element of 𝐺𝑛 is a word of at most L = 𝑂((𝑛 + 1)7 ) letters 𝑋𝑘 (the proof gives
     the exponent 3; we state 7 as in the formal development).
Proof. We take the sets 𝑉𝑗 to be quotients of 𝐺𝑛 by a chain of subgroups of 𝐾𝑛 . Choose
subgroups {1} = Γ0 ⊂ · · · ⊂ Γ𝐽 of 𝐾𝑛 , each of index 𝑏 in the next, with [𝐾𝑛 : Γ𝐽 ] < 𝑏, which
is possible in a 2-group. Let 𝑉𝑗 = 𝐺𝑛 /Γ𝑗 , with 𝐺𝑛 acting from the left, and let 𝜋 be the
natural projections.
   (a): Let 𝑃 𝑒𝑘 = 𝑒𝑘+1 for 𝑘 ̸= ℎ − 1 and 𝑃 𝑒ℎ−1 = 𝜍 −1 𝑒0 . Then 𝑔 ↦→ 𝑃 𝑔𝑃 −1 is an
automorphism of 𝐺𝑛 that maps 𝐾𝑛 to itself and 𝑋𝑘 to 𝑋𝑘+1 for every 𝑘 ∈ Z/ℎ, so each of
these groups is the image of 𝑈ℎ , the group generated by the 𝑋𝑘 with 𝑘 ̸= 0, under a power
of it. Now 𝑈ℎ meets the normal subgroup 𝐾𝑛 trivially, hence so do its images, and they act
freely because the stabilizers of the points of 𝑉𝑗 lie in 𝐾𝑛 .
   (b): Modulo 𝑠 the element 𝑋𝑖 is not in the group generated by the others, because of its
entry (𝑖 − 1, 𝑖), so, as stabilizers lie in 𝐾𝑛 , the elements 𝑐 and 𝑋𝑖 𝑐 lie in different cosets of
type 𝑖. As 𝜋 is equivariant and both actions are free, it maps a coset of type 𝑖 bijectively
onto one, and the preimage of a coset of type 𝑖 has 𝑏2 elements and consists of cosets of type
𝑖, hence of 𝑏 of them.
                                                                                               2
   (c): We have |𝑉𝐽 | = |SLℎ (F2 )| [𝐾𝑛 : Γ𝐽 ] with [𝐾𝑛 : Γ𝐽 ] < 𝑏, and |𝐺𝑛 | = |SLℎ (F2 )| 2(ℎ −1)𝑛 .
   (d): Write 𝑒𝑗𝑘 (𝑟) = 1 + 𝑟𝐸𝑗𝑘 . The 𝑒𝑗𝑘 (1) with 𝑗 < 𝑘 belong to 𝑈ℎ , the group generated
without 𝑋0 = 𝑒ℎ−1,0 (𝜍). As ℎ ≥ 3, the rule [𝑒𝑗𝑘 (𝑟), 𝑒𝑘𝑙 (𝑟′ )] = 𝑒𝑗𝑙 (𝑟𝑟′ ) gives from these and
𝑋0 all 𝑒𝑗𝑘 (𝜍) by words of bounded length, and then, by halving 𝑎, all 𝑒𝑗𝑘 (𝜍 𝑎 ) by words of
length 𝑂(𝑎2 ). The powers 𝜍, . . . , 𝜍 𝑛+1 span 𝑅𝑛 , and Gaussian elimination over this local ring
writes every matrix of 𝐺𝑛 as a product of boundedly many elementary matrices 𝑒𝑗𝑘 (𝑟). This
gives words of length 𝑂((𝑛 + 1)3 ), which is stronger than (d).                                    □

Formalization note. The exponent 7 in part (d) is the one that is proved in the Lean devel-
opment; the argument above gives the exponent 3.
   An edge of type 𝑖 of 𝑉𝑗 is a pair {𝑐, 𝑋𝑖 𝑐}, and the edges inside a coset of type 𝑖 are those of
the other types. For each coset 𝜔 of type 𝑖 of 𝑉𝑗+1 we fix a bijection 𝜒 ↦→ 𝜒  ^ from the 𝑏 cosets
of type 𝑖 of 𝑉𝑗 over 𝜔 to the 𝑏 elements of 𝜔. These groups, with the subgroups generated by
all but one of the 𝑋𝑘 , are close to those of the coset complexes of Kaufman and Oppenheim
[KO18, KO23].

H.2. Packets, networks and the two moves. From now on wires are moved in packets,
strings of ℓ bits for a fixed ℓ ≥ 1. A string 𝑤 is written on packets as 𝑤10 · · · 0, so it takes
⌊|𝑤|/ℓ⌋ + 1 of them, and 𝑤 can be recovered from them. A network on a finite set of nodes
assigns a number of packets to every pair of nodes, and to each node a node tensor, a function,
with values in a ring, of the bits of the packets at this node. Its value is the sum, over all
contents of all packets, of the product of the node tensors. This is a tensor network in
the usual sense [MS08b, Orús14], and tensor networks as a model of algebraic computation
are studied in [DHM02, CDM16, AKK22]. There are two kinds of moves, neither of which
changes the value.
Definition (relay). A relay through a node 𝑤 replaces a packet between two other nodes 𝑥
and 𝑦 by a packet between 𝑥 and 𝑤 and one between 𝑤 and 𝑦, with a test at 𝑤 that these
two are equal. It leaves no copy of the old packet, as the node tensors of 𝑥 and 𝑦 read the
new packets instead.
Definition (law move). Let 𝜔 be a coset of type 𝑖 in some 𝑉𝑗 whose elements are nodes of
a network, identified with 𝑈ℎ by 𝑔 ↦→ 𝑔𝑧0 for one of its elements 𝑧0 , so that the edges of 𝑈ℎ
become edges {𝑐, 𝑋𝑘 𝑐}. Let a law and a number 𝑡 ∈ N be given, and let 𝑡 packets of the
network be given to every slot of the law, where slots that are not full get packets of zeros.
The law move uses up these packets and applies (G.2) to each of the 𝑡ℓ layers of bits. By the
convention of Section G, every channel then holds a string of 𝑡ℓ blocks, each of which is the
code of an index followed by its bits. The string of every channel 𝑒 is written on 𝑜𝑒 + 1 new
packets, where 𝑜𝑒 = ⌊(length of the string)/ℓ⌋, and the vector 𝑜 = (𝑜𝑒 ) is called the outcome.
   A packet that is used up leaves one copy at each of its two ends, and each of the two nodes
sums over its own copy. The node tensor of the law move at an element of 𝜔 is the sum,
over one index for each layer and over the bits of its channels in each layer, of the products
of the node tensors of the law, restricted to those choices whose strings are the contents of
the new packets.
   Thus a law move replaces a network by a sum, over outcomes, of networks with 𝑜𝑒 + 1
packets on the channel 𝑒, whose node tensors check the codes, and the value does not change
because each choice of indices and bits belongs to
                                                 √ exactly one outcome. We call an outcome
of a law move with the number 𝑡 good if ‖𝑜‖* ≤ 𝐶1 𝑎1 𝑡 and 𝑜𝑒 ≥ 𝑡 for all channels 𝑒. Every
outcome that can occur is good, by Theorem G.2(b), the triangle inequality over the 𝑡ℓ layers,
and 𝜆 ≥ 1, so the sum may be restricted to good outcomes. The numbers of packets in the
new networks depend only on the outcomes, never on the contents of packets, and all bounds
below follow from such numbers.
H.3. The decomposition theorem. Of the ℎ types of edges of 𝑉𝑗 , only the packets on the
least loaded one travel on to 𝑉𝑗+1 , whereas all others end at a parent (Figure 11). Rerouting
the former multiplies their load by a factor that does not depend on ℎ, so by (G.1) the load,
in a quadratic norm, is halved at every stage, while the number of elements falls by the
constant factor 𝑏, and this gives the factor 𝑀 −𝛾 . Different outcomes of the law moves give
different trees. Parts (c), (d) and (e) of the theorem are needed only in Section J and can
be skipped at first reading.
Theorem H.2 (decomposition into tree networks). There is a constant 𝐶 with the following
property. Let 𝑀 ≥ 2, let ℓ, 𝑁 ≥ 1, and let a network over a commutative 𝐴-algebra, with
packets of ℓ bits, have at most 𝑀 nodes, no packet from a node to itself, and at most 𝑁 packets
at each node. Its value is the sum, over a set of choices o, each of which is a sequence of
good outcomes, of the values of networks with the following properties. Put Λ = (lg 𝑀 )𝐶 ,
where lg 𝑀 = ⌊log 𝑀 ⌋ + 1, and 𝛾 = 1/ log 𝑏.
 (a) The nodes form a rooted tree of depth at most Λ, by stages: the given nodes are of stage
     0, every node but the root has its parent in the next stage and at most 𝑏 children, and
     the root has at most 𝐶 children.
46                                         CLAUDE

 (b) A set of at most 𝑀 𝐶 named packets, the dead ones, may join any two nodes, and not all
     of them need be present. All other packets join a node and its parent. Their number is
     at most Λ𝑀 1−𝛾 (𝑁 + 𝑀 2 ) for one node, its children included, and at most Λ𝑀 (𝑁 + 𝑀 2 )
     in all.
 (c) The node tensor of a node is the given one (or 1) times the node tensors of the moves
     at it, summed over the packets that they have used up. At stage 0 these are tests only.
     Elsewhere they are tests and entries of node tensors of laws, and the numbers of layers
     of the law moves at a node add up to at most the number of bits of all packets that it
     ever holds.
 (d) The parents, the packets of the new network, the packets and numbers of the moves,
     and which outcomes are good are computed from o and the given numbers of packets
     by a straight-line program with at most (𝑀 𝑁 )𝐶 steps on numbers below (𝑀 𝑁 )𝐶 . A
     step is an input, a constant, or an arbitrary function of two earlier results, given by its
     table (a binary operation). The program does not depend on the numbers of packets,
     the outcomes or the node tensors. The numbers of nodes, of moves and of packets that
     can occur are at most (𝑀 𝑁 )𝐶 as well.
 (e) The program also computes for every node 𝑝 a number 𝑢𝑝 that bounds the number of
     packets, other than dead ones, between 𝑝 and its parent. The bounds of (b) hold with
     these numbers in place of those packets: 𝑢𝑝 and the numbers 𝑢𝑞 of the children 𝑞 of 𝑝
     add up to at most Λ𝑀 1−𝛾 (𝑁 + 𝑀 2 ), and all 𝑢𝑝 together to at most Λ𝑀 (𝑁 + 𝑀 2 ). The
     number of all packets that a node 𝑝 ever holds is at most a constant times the sum of
     𝑢𝑝 , of the numbers 𝑢𝑞 of its children 𝑞, and of the number of dead packets at 𝑝, present
     or not, and Λ𝑁 more if 𝑝 is a given node. Here the packets that are used up at 𝑝, by
     relays as well as by law moves, and the zeros in slots are counted too.

Formalization note. For part (d), and for the numbers 𝑢𝑝 of part (e), the proof below only
describes the straight-line program and accounts for its size. That the program computes
exactly what is claimed is proved in the Lean development (Appendix Q), and so are the
details of the count in part (e).

of that size, because 𝐶0′ is an integer. Packets are assigned to batches, slots and channels

                       Appendix I. Programs, automata and networks
   To apply Theorem H.2 to computations, we fix in this section what a computation is and
show that it is a network. Programs on binary trees (Section I.1) can run other programs,
as Section K requires, and the values of tape automata (Section I.2) are sums of values of
networks (Lemma I.2). Proposition I.1 connects the two models, and its proof, which follows
classical ideas, can be skipped at first reading. We keep the ring 𝐴 and the laws of Section G,
and lg 𝑣 = ⌊log 𝑣⌋ + 1 for an integer 𝑣 ≥ 1.

I.1. Programs and sums over runs. All data are binary trees, where a tree is either nil
or a pair of two trees, and the size |𝑥| of a tree 𝑥 is its number of nodes. A program is an
expression in the constructs of the following table, in which 𝑝1 , 𝑝2 , 𝑝3 stand for subprograms
and 𝑥 for the argument. Programs are nondeterministic, and a run carries a weight in 𝐴,
which is 1 at the start, and a tally, which is a list of names.
     construct                    output, and effect on the run
     a fixed tree; the argument   that tree; 𝑥
     first, second part of 𝑝1     that part of the output of 𝑝1 , and nil for nil
     pair of 𝑝1 , 𝑝2              the pair of the two outputs
     if 𝑝1 then 𝑝2 else 𝑝3        the output of 𝑝2 if that of 𝑝1 is not nil, otherwise that of 𝑝3
     run 𝑝1 𝑝2                    the output of the program coded by the output of 𝑝1 at the output of 𝑝2
     guess; fail                  two runs, with outputs nil and (nil, nil); no accepting run
     weight 𝑝1                    nil; multiplies the weight by the generator of 𝐴 named by the output of 𝑝1
     use 𝑝1                       nil; appends the output of 𝑝1 , a name, to the tally
Subprograms are evaluated from left to right, their weights multiply and their tallies are
concatenated. Every construct costs one step, and the cost of a run is its number of steps.
Finitely many generators of 𝐴, one of which is −1, have names, and every other name has
the weight 0. A run is accepting if it does not fail, and its output is then the output of the
program.
  A program is coded as the pair of the number of its outermost construct, in unary, and
the codes of its parts. Conversely every tree is read as a program, with nil read as the fixed
                                           VP ̸= VNP                                          49

tree nil and a pair whose first component is not the number of a construct as fail. A loop
is a program that receives its own code in its argument and calls run on it.
   An instance 𝑧 is a pair of a program and an argument, and the tree nil, of size 1, also
counts as one, with program and argument nil. That all runs of an instance halt is defined
inductively along the evaluation: in a pair and in run both subprograms must halt, also
when the first has no accepting run, and a chosen branch, and the program that is run, must
halt for every accepting run of what precedes them.
Definition. An instance 𝑧 is 𝜏 -plain if all its runs halt and every accepting run has an empty
tally and costs at most 𝜏 . The sum sum(𝑧) ∈ 𝐴 of an instance is the sum of the weights of
its accepting runs.
   Any machine model with a self-interpreter of constant overhead should do, and polyloga-
rithmic factors are always absorbed by powers of the parameter 𝑣 of Sections J and K. The
construct use and the tally are needed only in Section K, where a program that simulates a
circuit over R cannot contain the real constants of the circuit and names them in the tally
instead (Definition K.1). Finally, the code of an instance as a word, or briefly its word, lists
one symbol for each node of the tree, in preorder.
I.2. Tape automata. The second model is a weighted finite automaton that also writes on
tapes.
Definition. A tape automaton over 𝐴 has finite sets of states, of tapes and of symbols, two
fixed states 𝑞 − and 𝑞 + , and for each letter a matrix over 𝐴 whose rows and columns are
indexed by the states. Each tape has two sides. A letter is either idle, or it writes one
symbol on one of the two sides of a tape, or it writes one symbol on the input. A sequence
of 𝑡 letters is matched on an input word if on every tape the two sides receive the same word
and the input receives the given word. The value of the automaton on an input word in 𝑡
steps is the sum, over all sequences of 𝑡 letters that are matched on this word, of the entry
(𝑞 − , 𝑞 + ) of the product of their matrices.
   One side of a tape is for writing and the other for reading: a letter on the reading side
guesses the symbol that is read, and being matched forces the guesses to agree with what
is written. So a tape is like a queue, except that a symbol may be read before it is written.
One tape automaton, the universal automaton, evaluates every plain instance with a small
loss of time.
Proposition I.1 (a universal tape automaton). There are a tape automaton and a constant
𝐶univ such that the value of the automaton on the code of an instance 𝑧 as a word, in 𝑡 steps,
                                                         (︀           )︀2
is sum(𝑧) whenever 𝑧 is 𝜏 -plain and 𝑡 ≥ 𝐶univ (|𝑧| + 𝜏 ) lg(|𝑧| + 𝜏 ) .

Formalization note. The proof describes the automaton and counts its steps, and that the
automaton is exact is proved only in the Lean development (Appendix Q).

Proof. We describe the construction, which follows classical ideas [Sch78, Sch80, BEGKN94,
Rob91]: a pointer machine evaluates the program, and a tape automaton simulates it by
guessing what every load returns and checking the guesses at the end.
The pointer machine. The machine has a finite control and a fixed number of registers that
hold addresses, where the address 0 stands for nil. In one step it may load the pair at the
address in a register, allocate a pair of two registers at the next free address, test a register
for 0, guess a bit, or multiply by one of finitely many weights. It first reads the code of 𝑧,
which is the list of the brackets of the tree in preorder, and builds the tree, keeping a stack
of pairs in the heap. Then it evaluates the program on the argument, with a stack of frames
(tag, program, argument, outputs found so far) in the heap. On use it fails, since a plain
instance uses no name in an accepting run. The number of a construct is found by at most
11 loads, every construct takes boundedly many steps, and the name of a generator is read
50                                            CLAUDE


            input        part 1         part 2          part 3           part 4




           time          block 1        block 2         block 3         block 4



              𝑞0 = 𝑞 −             𝑞1             𝑞2               𝑞3             𝑞4 = 𝑞 +

        Figure 12. A run of a tape automaton over 𝐾𝑇 steps as a network, for

only to the bounded depth beyond which there are no names. Hence an accepting run of 𝑧 of
cost 𝑛 gives exactly one accepting run of the machine, with the same weight and 𝑂(|𝑧| + 𝑛)
steps, and there are no others.
The automaton. The automaton guesses the number 𝛽 of bits of an address and writes it
in unary on a tape that serves as a counter. The registers and the next free address are
words of 𝛽 bits that circulate on one tape: in each step of the machine they are read and
written again, and the free address is increased on the way by the carry rule, where a carry
out of the 𝛽 bits fails. For each load it guesses the pair and writes the record (address, load,
pair), and for each allocation, the input tree included, it writes (address, allocation, pair).
When the machine has accepted, it checks that the leading bit of the free address is 1, which
determines 𝛽, and it sorts the records by the key (address, allocations first). A pass of the
sort reads the list and writes each record on one of two tapes according to the first bit of its
key, with that bit moved to the end of the key, and then copies the first tape and after it
the second back to the list. The counter counts the 𝛽 + 1 passes, so the same tapes serve for
all of them. As the key is written with its least significant bit first, after 𝛽 + 1 passes it is in
its old order and the sort, which is stable, is complete. A last pass checks that each address
begins with an allocation and that all loads of it repeat its pair. After that the automaton
stays in its final state on idle letters, and this is the only place where idle letters are allowed.
Exactness and number of steps. Both sides of a tape carry the same word, and each block on
a tape ends with a mark and is written before it is read, so what is read is what was written,
in the same order. By induction on time all addresses in registers are below the free one, so
a load refers to the only allocation at its address, which is earlier. Hence the last pass forces
every guessed load, 𝛽 is forced, and all other letters are determined by the guesses of the
machine. Thus accepting runs correspond to matched sequences of length 𝑡, provided that 𝑡
is large enough. They need 𝑂(𝛽) letters for each of the 𝑂(|𝑧| + 𝜏 ) steps and records in each
of the 𝛽 + 1 passes, and 𝛽 = 𝑂(lg(|𝑧| + 𝜏 )). This is the classical conversion of random access
into sequential checking [GS89].                                                                  □
I.3. The runs of a tape automaton as a network. Cutting a computation into time
blocks and recording which block reads what another has written goes back to Hopcroft,
Paul and Valiant [HPV77] (see also [PPST83, Wil25]). For a tape automaton the result is
a network in the sense of Section H.2, whose nodes are the time blocks and the parts of the
input word (Figure 12). As a tape is a queue, the readers of what a node writes follow each
other in order, and the data 𝜌 below record the first and the last.
Lemma I.2 (the runs of a tape automaton as networks). Let 𝐾, 𝑇, ℓ ≥ 1 and 𝑇 ′ ≤ 𝑇 . Let
an input word of at most 𝐾𝑇 ′ symbols be cut into 𝐾 consecutive parts of 𝑇 ′ symbols, the
last parts possibly shorter or empty, and let the 𝐾𝑇 steps be cut into 𝐾 consecutive time
                                           VP ̸= VNP                                          51

blocks of 𝑇 steps. Order the 2𝐾 nodes as follows: first the 𝐾 time blocks, then the 𝐾 parts.
Regard the input as one more tape, which is written by the parts and read by the blocks. Let
𝑆 be the set of tapes and 𝑄 the set of states, and let the symbols and an end mark be coded
by 𝑐 bits each.
   ∙ Data 𝜌 say, for every tape and every node, whether the node writes nothing on the tape,
     and otherwise which is the first and which is the last node that reads its symbols.
   ∙ The data 𝜌 are valid if on every tape the first reader of a node is not before the last
     reader of the node before it, and not after the last reader of the node itself. Here a node
     that writes nothing repeats the last reader of the node before it, so that 𝜌 is determined
     by the sequence of letters, and for the first node, the first node itself takes the place of
     that last reader.
Then the value of the automaton in 𝐾𝑇 steps is the sum of the values of networks on these
2𝐾 nodes, one network for every choice of
   ∙ boundary states 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄𝐾+1 with 𝑞0 = 𝑞 − and 𝑞𝐾 = 𝑞 + ,
   ∙ valid data 𝜌, and
   ∙ numbers of packets of ℓ bits between any two nodes, each number at most (𝑇 + |𝑆| +
     1)𝑐/ℓ + 1.
                                                                                 (︀
In each of these networks a node whose node tensor is not 0 has at most 2 (𝑇 + 2𝐾(|𝑆| +
             )︀
1))𝑐/ℓ + 2𝐾 packets.

Proof. We describe the network of one choice and then compare its terms with the matched
sequences. For an ordered pair (𝑝, 𝑝′ ) of nodes, the symbols that 𝑝 writes and 𝑝′ reads on all
|𝑆| + 1 tapes, those of each tape closed by the end mark, form one string, which is written on
the packets from 𝑝 to 𝑝′ . On a tape, two nodes are partners if one reads a symbol that the
other writes. The node tensor of a part tests that the contents of its packets are its symbols.
The node tensor of the 𝑖th block is the sum, over all sequences of 𝑇 letters that have the
partners prescribed by 𝜌 and whose symbols, coded on packets, are the given contents, of
the entry (𝑞𝑖−1 , 𝑞𝑖 ) of the product of their matrices. It vanishes unless the block writes at
most 𝑇 symbols in all, which bounds its packets.
   In a matched sequence the two sides of a tape carry the same word, so the 𝑗th symbol of
one side meets the 𝑗th symbol of the other. Hence the partners of a node on a side follow
each other in order, from a first to a last, and every matched sequence belongs to one choice
of a valid 𝜌, of the boundary states and of the numbers of packets.
   Conversely, let 𝜌 be valid, and on some tape let 𝑤𝑝𝑝′ be the symbols that 𝑝 writes for 𝑝′ ,
in a term that is not 0. If 𝑤𝑝𝑝′ and 𝑤𝑟𝑟′ are not empty and 𝑝 < 𝑟, then 𝑝′ ≤ 𝑟′ , because 𝜌 is
valid. So the 𝑤𝑝𝑝′ give the same word in the order of (𝑝, 𝑝′ ), which is the writing side, and
in that of (𝑝′ , 𝑝), the reading side, and the sequence is matched.                          □


                   Appendix J. Cutting a computation into pieces
   Combining Theorem H.2 with Lemma I.2, we prove in this section that a long computation
can be cut into many short and almost independent ones, the pieces, and that a fast program
lists them (Theorem J.4). All quantities are measured in powers of a large integer 𝑣, which
we call the scale. Proposition J.2 is the combinatorial part, Theorem J.3 turns it into a
program that makes one cut, and Theorem J.4 repeats such cuts until the pieces take 𝑣 𝐻
steps, for a constant 𝐻. Table 2 lists the powers of 𝑣 used from here to the end of Section K;
the exponents are not optimized, and apart from the few inequalities noted in the table only
their order matters.

Convention. Throughout this section Λ denotes a power of lg 𝑣 whose exponent may grow
a bounded number of times. We write 𝐶tree for the constant 𝐶 of Theorem H.2, and 𝐶univ
is the constant of Proposition I.1.

J.1. Programs that cut.
Definition J.1. A program 𝐺 cuts (𝜏, 𝑠) into (𝜏 ′ , 𝑠′ ) with 𝑃 pieces in 𝑡 steps if the following
holds for every instance 𝑧 with |𝑧| ≤ 𝑠.
   ∙ All runs of 𝐺 on 𝑧 halt. The accepting ones take at most 𝑡 steps, have empty tallies,
     and output lists of at most 𝑃 instances of size at most 𝑠′ .
   ∙ If 𝑧 is 𝜏 -plain, then all these instances are 𝜏 ′ -plain, and
                                              ∑︁                    ∏︁
                                 sum(𝑧) =            weight ·                   sum(𝑦),
                                              runs              𝑦 in the list
     where the sum is over the accepting runs of 𝐺 on 𝑧.
  Thus every accepting run of 𝐺 outputs a list of instances, the pieces of 𝑧. The first
condition is required of every instance of size at most 𝑠, plain or not, which Proposition K.5
uses.
J.2. The pieces of a tree network. By Lemma I.2 and Theorem H.2 the value of a tape
automaton in 𝐾𝑇 steps is a sum of values of tree networks. The next proposition cuts
each tree into subtrees, the pieces, each with about the fraction 1/𝑘 of the nodes and of the
packets, for a prescribed 𝑘. Once the packets between different pieces are fixed, the sum over
all others is a product of one factor for each piece, its value.
Proposition J.2 (the pieces of a computation). For every tape automaton over 𝐴 there
are constants 𝑎, 𝑎𝐾 and 𝐶, where 𝑎𝐾 ≥ 300 and where 𝑎 and 𝑎𝐾 do not depend on the
automaton, with the following property. For all large 𝑣 there is 𝐾 with 𝑣 𝑎𝐾 ≤ 𝐾 ≤ 𝐶𝑣 𝑎𝐾
such that the statements below hold for all 𝑇 ≥ 𝑣 𝑎 with lg 𝑇 ≤ (lg 𝑣)2 and all 2 ≤ 𝑘 ≤ 𝑣 64 .
   Cut an input word of at most 𝐾𝑇 ′ symbols, where 𝑇 ′ ≤ 𝑇 , into 𝐾 consecutive parts of 𝑇 ′
symbols, the last parts possibly shorter or empty.
   ∙ The value of the automaton in 𝐾𝑇 steps is the sum, over boundary states, valid data 𝜌
     as in Lemma I.2, numbers of packets, good outcomes, and the contents of cut packets,
     of products of the values of at most 𝑘Λ pieces.
   ∙ A piece has at most 𝐶𝐾/𝑘 blocks and parts, at most 𝐾𝑇 Λ/𝑣 300 bits in cut packets, and
     at most 𝐾𝑇 Λ/𝑘 bits in all.
   ∙ The packets, the pieces and what each node tensor consists of are computed from the
     numbers of packets and the outcomes by 𝑣 𝐶 binary operations on numbers below 𝑣 𝐶 .
Formalization note. The last claim rests on Theorem H.2(d) and (e), whose printed proofs
only describe the program in question.

Heavy nodes and pieces. Let 𝑌 be the bound for the total in Theorem H.2(b), and let the
given nodes be the blocks and parts. A node is below the nodes in whose subtrees it lies,
itself included, and the packets below a node are those between a node below it and the
parent of that node, counted by the numbers 𝑢𝑝 of Theorem H.2(e). Call a node of the tree
heavy if it is the root or if

and light otherwise. The sets below different nodes of one stage are disjoint, so over a stage
the left sides add up to at most 14 + 12 , and each stage has fewer than 𝑘 heavy nodes. Parents
of heavy nodes are heavy. A piece is a heavy node, or the subtree of a light node with a
heavy parent, so there are at most 𝑘Λ pieces. The packets between different pieces are the
cut packets, and the value of a piece sums over its other packets, the inner ones. Every
assignment of packets and letters is counted once, since the contents of absent or padded
places are forced to be zero.
The bounds. By part (b) of the theorem a piece has at most Λ𝑀 1−𝛾 𝑁 ℓ ≤ 𝐾𝑇 Λ/𝑣 300 bits in

and 2𝑌 /𝑘 packets, because its root is light, and a single node has fewer still, as 𝑘 ≤ 𝑣 64 .
Packets used up and zeros in slots change this by a constant factor only, apart from Λ𝑁 at
each given node, by Theorem H.2(e). The packets from a node to itself are at most 𝑁 at
each of the at most 4𝑀/𝑘 given nodes, and the dead packets have at most 𝑇 /𝑣 300 bits. This
bounds the bits in all. Parts (d) and (e) of the theorem give the last claim, since the heavy
nodes and the pieces are computed from the parents and the numbers 𝑢𝑝 .                      □
J.3. One cut. The next theorem turns Proposition J.2 into a program. Each piece becomes
an instance again, whose program is a fixed evaluator and whose argument is a script that
describes the piece.
Theorem J.3 (one cut). There are constants 𝑎0 , 𝐶, 𝑣0 such that the following holds with
Λ = (lg 𝑣)𝐶 , for all 𝑣 ≥ 𝑣0 , all 𝜏 ≥ 𝑣 𝑎0 with 4 lg 𝜏 ≤ (lg 𝑣)2 , all 𝑠 ≤ 𝜏 and all 2 ≤ 𝑘 ≤ 𝑣 64 .
Some program of size at most 𝑣 𝑎0 cuts (𝜏, 𝑠) into
                                         𝜏 Λ/𝑘, (𝑠/𝑘 + 𝜏 /𝑣 256 )Λ

with at most 𝑘Λ pieces, in (𝑠 + 𝜏 𝑘/𝑣 256 )Λ steps.
Formalization note. The proof describes the evaluator and the cutting program 𝐺 but does

   Thus a cut by 𝑘 divides the time and the input by 𝑘, up to the factor Λ, and the term
𝜏 /𝑣 256 accounts for the cut packets and the description of the tree, which the input of a piece

Proof. We obtain a formula for sum(𝑧) from Proposition J.2 and then describe the two

54                                             CLAUDE

of the tower among them, are smaller than 𝑣 𝑎0 and than 𝜏 /𝑣 256 . The proposition therefore
gives the formula for sum(𝑧). A piece reads at most (𝑠/𝑘 + 𝑀/𝑘)Λ symbols of the input,
and 𝑀 ≤ 𝜏 /𝑣 256 .
The evaluator. Call the tree, the packets and the pieces of one choice its plan. We name
its packets, and the places of letters in blocks, by strings of 𝑂((lg 𝑣)2 ) bits, and a binary
trie finds an object in that many steps. A script of a piece contains the numbers 𝑇 and
ℓ in binary, the contents of its cut packets, the names of its inner packets, and a list of
instructions of three kinds.
   ∙ Block: the boundary states of a block, its entries of 𝜌, the numbers of packets, and
      the names of the packets that carry them (or, for the input, the symbols themselves).
   ∙ Role, which is the part of one node in one law move: the law, the number 𝑡, the
      outcome, and for every slot and channel at this node the names of the packets that
      supply or receive its bits. A slot may be supplied by zeros. The bits of a segment that
      a law has replaced are those of the packet on the other side of the test at the node.
   ∙ Test: two packets are equal, or one packet is zero.
The evaluator guesses the contents of all inner packets and stores them. For Block it guesses
𝑇 letters and the states between them, multiplies for each by the matrix entry, collects the
symbols of every side, checks that they fit these partners and numbers, and compares them,
coded on packets, with the stored contents. For Role it guesses the index of each of the
𝑡ℓ layers and the bits of its channels, reads the bits of the slots in order, multiplies by the
entry of the node tensor, and compares the coded strings with the stored packets. An entry
of a matrix or of a node tensor is an element of 𝐴, hence a fixed sum of products of the
generators, so multiplying by it is guessing a summand and multiplying by its factors. The
contents fix the symbols and the indices, since codes are injective, and what is still summed
over is exactly what the node tensors of Lemma I.2 sum over. Hence the sum of the instance
(evaluator, script) is the value of the piece. All its loops have lengths that stand in the script,
so all its runs halt, and within
               #blocks · 𝑇 + #bits of packets + #instructions · 𝑣 𝑂(1) (lg 𝑣)𝑂(1) ≤ 𝜏 Λ/𝑘
          (︀                                                          )︀

steps, because 𝑎0 is large and because 𝑡 ≤ 𝑜𝑒 , which holds for every channel 𝑒 of a good
outcome 𝑜 (Section H.2), bounds the layers by the bits of the packets.
The program 𝐺. The program contains the tables of the tower. On 𝑧 it writes the word of 𝑧
and cuts it into 𝐾 parts of equal length, the last ones shorter. It guesses 𝑞, 𝜌, the numbers of
packets and the outcomes, each in binary with a fixed number of digits, and it rejects unless
𝜌 is valid, the numbers give every node at most 𝑁 packets, and all outcomes are good. With
these it computes by the binary operations, in (𝑀 𝑁 )𝐶tree Λ steps, the tree, the packets, the
heavy nodes and the pieces, where rules such as ranks in a fixed enumeration decide which
packet goes where. It then guesses the contents of the cut packets, writes for each piece its
script, and outputs the list of the instances (evaluator, script). All of this is deterministic
except for the guesses, and every choice in the formula is guessed exactly once, so the sum
formula is the one obtained above.
   A script has (𝑠/𝑘 + 𝑀/𝑘)Λ input symbols, 𝜏 Λ/𝑣 300 bits of cut packets, and 𝑣 𝑂(1) names
and instructions, which is at most (𝑠/𝑘 + 𝜏 /𝑣 256 )Λ in all. The program 𝐺 takes 𝑠Λ steps
for the word, (𝑀 𝑁 )𝐶tree Λ ≤ 𝜏 Λ/𝑣 256 steps for the plan, and 𝑘Λ · 𝜏 Λ/𝑣 300 steps for the cut
packets, which gives the stated size of the pieces and number of steps.                       □

J.4. Cuts at all scales. We now repeat the cut of Theorem J.3 until the pieces take 𝑣 𝐻
steps, and the point to watch is that the inputs remain shorter than the times by the factor
𝑣 48 , up to a factor 4 for each cut.
Theorem J.4 (cuts at all scales). There are a multiple 𝐻 ≥ 272 of 16 and constants 𝐶cut , 𝑣0
such that the following holds whenever 𝑣 ≥ 𝑣0 and 𝛼 satisfy (lg 𝑣)𝐶cut 𝛼 ≤ 𝑣. For all 𝜏 and

𝑠 with 𝑣 𝐻 ≤ 𝜏 ≤ 𝑣 𝐻𝛼 and 𝑠𝑣 48 ≤ 𝜏 , some program of size at most 𝑣 𝐻 cuts (𝜏, 𝑠) into
(𝑣 𝐻 , 𝑣 𝐻−32 ) with at most 𝜏 𝑣 2 /𝑣 𝐻 pieces in at most 𝜏 /𝑣 24 steps.
Proof. We compose cuts of Theorem J.3 and follow their parameters by induction. Let 𝐻 be
a multiple of 16 with 𝐻 ≥ 272 and 𝐻 ≥ 𝑎0 + 16. We cut by 𝑘 = 𝑣 63 as long as 𝜏 Λ ≥ 𝑣 𝐻+64 ,
and at last once by 𝑘 = ⌊𝜏 Λ/𝑣 𝐻 ⌋ + 1, where 𝜏 is the current bound on the time, and we
compose the programs, so that the next one is run on every piece in the list. The condition
on 𝑣 gives 4 lg 𝜏 ≤ (lg 𝑣)2 , and there are 𝑟 = 𝑂(𝛼) cuts.

after the last cut, for which 𝑘 ≤ 2𝜏𝑟−1 Λ/𝑣 𝐻 . Three bounds hold by induction on 𝑖, starting
from 𝑠𝑣 48 ≤ 𝜏 . First, 𝑠𝑖 𝑣 48 ≤ 4𝑖 𝜏𝑖 , because 𝑘 ≤ 𝑣 64 and 48 + 64 ≤ 256, so that Theorem J.3
applies again. Second, 𝑃𝑖 𝜏𝑖 ≤ 𝜏 (4Λ2 )𝑖 . Third, the next cut takes at most (𝑠𝑖 + 𝜏𝑖 𝑘/𝑣 256 )Λ ≤
2 · 4𝑖 𝜏𝑖 Λ/𝑣 48 steps on each piece. Moreover, the sum formulas compose.
    It follows that the final pieces have size at most 𝑣 𝐻−48 (8Λ)𝑟 and that there are at most
(𝜏 /𝑣 𝐻 )(4Λ2 )𝑟 of them. The number of steps is at most (𝜏 /𝑣 48 )(100Λ3 )𝑟 , and the program
has size at most 4𝑟 (𝑣 𝑎0 + 𝑂(1)). All these powers of Λ are below 𝑣 if 𝐶cut is large, which
gives the bounds of the theorem.                                                                 □

Theorem L.2 needs.

         Appendix K. Small circuits would speed up every computation
  We are still in the contracting case, and we keep the ring 𝐴 of Theorem G.2 and the
constant 𝐻 of Theorem J.4, and put ℎ0 = 𝐻/16. In this section we prove Theorem K.7, the
lower bound for the permanent in the contracting case. A small circuit for the permanent
would give a program, called an accelerator, that evaluates sums over the runs of other
programs in fewer steps than those runs take, and we show that accelerators do not exist.

K.1. Accelerators. A circuit for PERM𝑚 with 𝑚𝐶 gates is advice that is longer than the
running times at its own scale, so we cannot diagonalize against it there. By Theorem J.4,
however, a device that speeds up sums over runs of 𝑣 𝐻 steps also speeds up much longer
sums, piece by piece. The same advice therefore serves at the scale 𝑣 ′ = 𝑣 𝛼 , where it is short
(about 𝑣 ′𝐻/4 , against inputs of length 𝑣 ′𝐻−32 ) and a diagonal argument applies.
   A run of a program never multiplies by the constants of a circuit but only names them in
                                      𝑖
its tally (Section I). The powers 𝜉 2 of a constant 𝜉 also get names, so that a program can
                               𝑖
multiply by 𝜉 𝑒 by naming 𝜉 2 for the binary digits of 𝑒 that are 1.
Definition K.1. Let 𝑅 be a commutative 𝐴-algebra. An accelerator at scale 𝑣 with con-
stants 𝜉1 , 𝜉2 , . . . from 𝑅 and exponent bound 𝐵 is a program 𝐷 with the following two
properties.
  (i) On every instance 𝑧 with |𝑧| ≤ 𝑣 𝐻−32 all runs of 𝐷 halt, the accepting runs halt within
                                                                                                  𝑖
       𝑣 𝐻−16 steps, and every name in the tally of an accepting run stands for a power 𝜉𝑙2
       with 𝑖 < 𝐵.
 (ii) If moreover 𝑧 is 𝑣 𝐻 -plain, then the value of 𝐷 on 𝑧 is sum(𝑧), where the value is the
       sum, over the accepting runs, of the weight of the run times the powers that its tally
       names, each taken as often as it is named.
The constants sit at the leaves of a binary trie with 𝑄 nodes, and names are paths: the name
      𝑖
of 𝜉𝑙2 is the path from the root to the leaf of 𝜉𝑙 , followed by 1𝑖 0. The accelerator is of size 𝑠
if 𝑄 and the size of 𝐷 are at most 𝑠 and, with 𝛽 = lg(max(𝑣 𝐻 , 𝑠)), the height of the trie is
at most 𝛽 2 and 𝐵 ≤ 𝛽 3 .
  An instance of size at most 𝑣 𝐻−32 is called short, and Table 2 explains the margins between
the exponents 𝐻, 𝐻 − 16 and 𝐻 − 32.
56                                                        CLAUDE

K.2. Accelerators from small circuits. The first lemma evaluates the part of degree at
most 𝑑 of a polynomial with a small circuit in about 𝑑 + |𝑧| steps, up to a polylogarithmic
factor. The polynomials have one variable for every pair (place, symbol), where an end mark
counts as a symbol. The indicator assignment of a word of fewer than 𝑛 symbols sets to 1
the variable (place, symbol) for each of its symbols and the variable of the end mark at the
place after the last symbol, and it sets all other variables to 0.
Lemma K.2 (evaluation of the part of low degree). Let 𝑅 be a commutative 𝐴-algebra, let
𝑑 ≥ 0, and let a polynomial over 𝑅 in the variables (place, symbol), with 𝑛 places, have a
circuit with 𝐿 gates. There is a program of size 𝑂(log 𝐿+log 𝑛+𝑑), with 𝑂((𝐿+𝑛)3 ) constants,
which on every instance 𝑧 with |𝑧| < 𝑛 has as value, in (𝑑 + |𝑧| + 1)(log(𝐿 + 𝑛 + 𝑑))𝑂(1) steps,
the sum of the homogeneous parts of degree at most 𝑑 of the polynomial, evaluated at the
indicator assignment of the word of 𝑧. All its runs halt, it names only constants, not powers,
and the trie that holds the constants has height 𝑂(log 𝐿 + log 𝑛).
Proof. We unfold a recursion for the homogeneous parts at the gates whose coefficients do
not depend on the degree. Let 𝑔 (𝑖) be the homogeneous part of degree 𝑖 of the polynomial
at the gate 𝑔. For 𝑖 ≥ 1 we have
      (𝑔 ′ + 𝑔 ′′ )(𝑖) = 𝑔 ′(𝑖) + 𝑔 ′′(𝑖) ,    (𝑔 ′ 𝑔 ′′ )(𝑖) = 𝑔 ′(0) 𝑔 ′′(𝑖) + 𝑔 ′′(0) 𝑔 ′(𝑖) +                   𝑔 ′(𝑗) 𝑔 ′′(𝑖−𝑗) .

A chain goes from a gate to one of the two gates below it (in two ways if these are the same
gate), and so on. A step has the factor 1 at a sum gate, and at a product gate the part of
degree zero of the other gate below. Let 𝑐𝑔,𝛾 ∈ 𝑅 be the sum, over all chains from 𝑔 to 𝛾,
of the products of their factors, with 𝑐𝑔,𝑔 = 1. Let 𝑎𝑔,𝑙 be the sum of the 𝑐𝑔,𝛾 over the input
gates 𝛾 with the variable 𝑥𝑙 , and 𝑏𝑔,𝑔′ ,𝑔′′ that over the product gates 𝛾 = 𝑔 ′ 𝑔 ′′ . By induction

These constants do not depend on 𝑖, and as they are indexed by a gate, a place and a symbol,
or by three gates, names of 𝑂(log 𝐿 + log 𝑛) bits suffice. One more constant is the part of
degree zero of the output gate.
   The program writes the word of 𝑧 as a balanced tree, so that the symbol at a place can be
read in 𝑂(log 𝑛) steps. If 𝑑 = 0, it names the last constant and accepts. Otherwise it guesses
a bit, and for one value it again names that constant and accepts. For the other value it
keeps a stack of gates, at first the output gate only, and a counter, at first 𝑑 − 1 in unary. In
each round it takes a gate 𝑔 from the stack and guesses either a variable 𝑥𝑙 , in which case
it names 𝑎𝑔,𝑙 and rejects if 𝑥𝑙 = 0 at the indicator assignment, or, if the counter is not 0,
two gates 𝑔 ′ , 𝑔 ′′ , in which case it names 𝑏𝑔,𝑔′ ,𝑔′′ , puts both gates on the stack and decreases
the counter. It accepts when the stack is empty. Every round removes a gate for good or
decreases the counter, so all runs halt after at most 2𝑑 − 1 rounds of (log(𝐿 + 𝑛))𝑂(1) steps.
The accepting runs are, in preorder, the parse trees with at most 𝑑 leaves [JS82, MP08]
(compare [VSBR83, Vin91, AJMV98]), and the products of the constants that they name
sum to 1≤𝑖≤𝑑 𝑔 (𝑖) at the indicator assignment, where 𝑔 is the output gate.

   We apply Lemma K.2 to a polynomial Φ whose values at indicator assignments are the
sums sum(𝑧) of the short plain instances, and which is a clause sum, so that by Corollary E.2
it has a small circuit if the permanent has one.
Lemma K.3 (accelerators from circuits for the permanent). There are constants 𝐵0 , 𝐶, 𝑣0
such that the following holds for all integers 𝑎 ≥ 0 and 𝑣 ≥ 𝑣0 with (lg 𝑣)𝐶𝑎 ≤ 𝑣. Let 𝑅 be a
field that is an 𝐴-algebra and in which 2 ̸= 0. If, for some 𝑚 ≥ 𝑣 𝐵0 , the polynomial PERM𝑚
has a circuit over 𝑅 with at most 𝑣 𝑎 gates, then 𝑅 has an accelerator of size 𝑣 𝐶(𝑎+1) at scale
𝑣.
                                             VP ̸= VNP                                          57

Proof. Let 𝑛 = 𝑣 𝐻−32 + 1, so that the word of a short instance has fewer than 𝑛 symbols.
Let Φ be the sum, over all words 𝑤 of fewer than 𝑛 symbols, of the value of the universal
automaton on 𝑤 in 𝑡 = 𝑣 𝐻+1 steps times the product of the variables that are 1 at the
indicator assignment of 𝑤. It has degree at most 𝑛, and at the indicator assignment of a
word it takes the value of the automaton on that word, because no indicator assignment
contains another.
   It is a clause sum, whose bits are the indicator assignment of 𝑤, for every step a one-hot
code of the state and of the letter, for every step and every side of a tape a one-hot counter of
the symbols written so far, and for every tape and place a one-hot code of a symbol. Clauses
of weight 0 enforce one-hotness, the first and the last state, and the single end mark of 𝑤.
They also forbid a wrong count, a symbol written at count 𝑖 that differs from the symbol at
place 𝑖, different final counts on the two sides of a tape, and symbols beyond the final count.
The other clauses carry the entries of the matrices of the automaton and the variables. So
there is exactly one assignment for each matched sequence and each sequence of states. The
clause sum has 𝑣 𝑂(1) literals, and we choose 𝐵0 so that 𝑣 𝐵0 is at least three times their
number. By Corollary E.2 a circuit for PERM𝑚 with at most 𝑣 𝑎 gates then gives one for Φ
with 𝑂(𝑣 𝑎 ) gates.
   The word of an instance uses only two symbols, so we set the variables of all other symbols,
the end mark excepted, to 0. By Proposition I.1 the polynomial Φ then takes the value sum(𝑧)
at the indicator assignment of the word of every short 𝑣 𝐻 -plain instance 𝑧. As 𝑛 bounds the
degree of Φ, Lemma K.2 with 𝑑 = 𝑛 turns the circuit into a program with 𝑣 𝑂(𝑎+1) constants
that has these values and takes 𝑣 𝐻−32 ((𝑎 + 1) lg 𝑣)𝑂(1) ≤ 𝑣 𝐻−16 steps. All its runs halt, its
names have 𝑂((𝑎 + 1) lg 𝑣) bits, and 𝐵 = 1, so it is an accelerator of size 𝑣 𝐶(𝑎+1) if 𝐶 is large.
The proof uses of the permanent only that Corollary E.2 holds for it, so the lemma holds for
every family that is complete for grid sums (Appendix M), with constants that depend on
the factor of the family, which takes the place of 3 in Corollary E.2.                           □
K.3. Transferring accelerators between scales. The tool for passing to larger scales is
Theorem J.4, but it applies only to plain instances, whose accepting runs name no constants,
whereas the programs to be sped up contain the accelerator. The following lemma therefore
collects the constants in front of the sum over the runs.
Lemma K.4 (postponing the constants). Let 𝑝 be a program and let an argument be given
on which all runs of 𝑝 halt, the accepting ones within 𝑇 < 2𝐽 steps and with names among
the leaves of a binary trie with 𝑄′ nodes and height 𝜂. Let the leaves of the trie stand for
powers 𝜆1 , 𝜆2 , . . . Then the value of 𝑝 on the argument is
                                ∑︁                          ∏︁ 𝑒𝑗
                                     𝜆𝑒 sum(𝑧𝑒 ),    𝜆𝑒 =       𝜆𝑗 ,
                                 𝑒                          𝑗

where 𝑒 runs over the vectors with one entry in {0, . . . , 2𝐽 −1} for every leaf, and the instance
𝑧𝑒 accepts exactly the runs of 𝑝 whose tally uses each name the prescribed number of times.
The instance 𝑧𝑒 is plain within 𝑂(𝑇 (𝐽 + 𝜂 + 1) + 𝑄′ (𝐽 + 1)) steps, and its size is that of 𝑝
and the argument plus 𝑂(𝑄′ (𝐽 + 1)).
Proof. We take 𝑧𝑒 = (𝐼, (𝑝, argument, 𝑒)), where 𝐼 is a fixed program, the interpreter. It
evaluates 𝑝 construct by construct, also inside run, with constant overhead, and it carries 𝑒
as a binary trie of counters indexed by the names. At a use it follows the name and decreases
that counter, and it fails if there is none or if it is 0, which takes 𝑂(𝐽 + 𝜂 + 1) steps. The
interpreter accepts if 𝑝 accepts and all counters are 0 at the end. Hence it halts on every
run and has empty tallies. An accepting run of 𝑝 is accepted for exactly one 𝑒, because the
numbers of times that it uses the names are at most 𝑇 < 2𝐽 .                                □
   The next proposition is stated with explicit bounds, so that it can be applied repeatedly.
Proposition K.5 (transfer to the next scale). Let 𝑣 be large, let 𝛼 ≥ 5 be an integer, and
suppose that (lg 𝑣)𝐶𝛼 ≤ 𝑣, where 𝐶 is a suitable constant that depends on the fixed power

below. Let 𝐷 be a program with the two properties of an accelerator at scale 𝑣 (Definition K.1),

program with the two properties of an accelerator at scale 𝑣 ′ = 𝑣 𝛼 , with the same constants,
with exponent bound 𝐵 + 51ℎ0 𝛼2 lg 𝑣 and with program size at most 𝑣 ′4ℎ0 . Its accepting runs
take at most 𝑣 ′𝐻−17 steps. For the new program we claim only this, and not that it is an
accelerator of a given size in the sense of Definition K.1, which would require the bounds of
that definition on the height of the trie and on the exponent bound.
Proof. We speed up the universal program run in rounds, each of which lowers the exponent
of 𝑣 in the running time by 8. Let 𝐽 = (𝐻𝛼+1) lg 𝑣, so that 2𝐽 exceeds all running times, and
let 𝑋 bound 𝐽, the lengths of the names and all exponent bounds below. By the hypothesis
every fixed power of 𝑋 is less than 𝑣. We construct programs 𝑝 that have the values required
at scale 𝑣 ′ in 𝑣 𝑓 +8 steps, starting with the program run and 𝑓 + 8 = 𝐻𝛼 + 1.

Halting. A new program only writes 𝑧𝑒′ down, and the cutting program and 𝐷 halt on
all short instances, plain or not, by the first condition of Definition J.1 and property (i) of
Definition K.1. Thus the new program halts whether or not 𝑝 does. The construction is not
circular, because 𝐷 is one fixed program that is right on all short instances.                       □

K.4. Nonexistence of accelerators. We now show, by a diagonal argument, that acceler-
ators of moderate size do not exist.
Theorem K.6 (nonexistence of small accelerators). There are 𝐶 and 𝑣1 such that for all
integers 𝑎 ≥ 0 and 𝑣 ≥ 𝑣1 with (lg 𝑣)𝐶𝑎 ≤ 𝑣 no nonzero commutative 𝐴-algebra has an
accelerator of size 𝑣 𝑎 at scale 𝑣.
Proof. Suppose that there is such an accelerator. We reduce its constants into a finite field,
pass to a large scale by Proposition K.5, and diagonalize there. As 𝐴 is finitely generated
(Theorem G.2), so is the ring that its constants generate over 𝐴. Modulo a maximal ideal this
ring is a field that is finitely generated as a ring, hence a finite field 𝐸 [Eis95, Theorem 4.19],
over which the program is still an accelerator, but we know nothing about the size of 𝐸.
All scales. Proposition K.5 with 𝛼 = max(5, 𝑎), and then again and again with 𝛼 = 5, gives
programs with the two properties of an accelerator at arbitrarily large scales 𝑣𝑖 , with the
same constants, of size at most 𝑣𝑖4ℎ0 and with exponent bounds 𝑂((lg 𝑣𝑖 )3 ). Its hypotheses
                                                                   ′
hold again, since 𝑄 ≤ 𝑣𝑖ℎ0 , the trie is the same, and (lg 𝑣𝑖 )5𝐶 ≤ 𝑣𝑖 , where 𝐶 ′ is the constant
of the proposition.

Remark. This is an indirect diagonalization in the sense of [FLMV05, vM07, SW14, MW17].
Compare the lower bounds for the permanent against uniform threshold circuits of Allender
[All99] and of Koiran and Perifel [KP09]. The assumed speed-up is used at one scale only.
K.5. The lower bound in the contracting case. The lower bound now follows from
Lemma K.3 and Theorem K.6 by a calculation.
Theorem K.7 (lower bound in the contracting case). In the contracting case there are
𝑐 > 0 and 𝑚0 such that for 𝑚 ≥ 𝑚0 every circuit over k for PERM𝑚 has more than
         2
2𝑐(log 𝑚) / log log 𝑚 gates.
Proof. Suppose, for a small 𝑐 > 0 and some large 𝑚, that a circuit over k for PERM𝑚 has
                 2
at most 2𝑐(log 𝑚) / log log 𝑚 gates. Let 𝑣 = ⌊𝑚1/𝐵0 ⌋, so that 𝑚 ≥ 𝑣 𝐵0 , and let 𝑎 be the least
integer such that the circuit has at most 𝑣 𝑎 gates, so that 𝑎 = 𝑂(𝑐 log 𝑚/ log log 𝑚) + 1. For
                                ′
any fixed 𝐶 ′ we have (lg 𝑣)𝐶 (𝑎+1) ≤ 𝑣 if 𝑐 is small enough and 𝑚 is large. Hence Lemma K.3
gives an accelerator of size 𝑣 𝐶(𝑎+1) at scale 𝑣 over k, which is a nonzero 𝐴-algebra, and this
contradicts Theorem K.6 with 𝐶(𝑎 + 1) in place of 𝑎.                                          □

                       Appendix L. Proofs of the main theorems
   Sections A to K work over the real numbers, and this section proves the following three
statements over R. Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2, which hold over every field, are then
proved in Appendix M.
Theorem L.1 (main theorem). There are 𝑐 > 0 and 𝑚0 such that for all 𝑚 ≥ 𝑚0 every
circuit over R that computes PERM𝑚 has more than
                                                 1/3             4/3
                                     𝑚𝑐(log 𝑚)         = 2𝑐(log 𝑚)
gates.
Theorem L.2 (no circuits of polynomial size). For every integer 𝐶 ≥ 0 there is 𝑚0 such
that for all 𝑚 ≥ 𝑚0 no circuit over R with at most 𝑚𝐶 gates computes PERM𝑚 .
Corollary L.3. Over R, VP is a proper subset of VNP.
  The flat case was treated in Section E and the contracting case in Sections F to K, and
by Theorem D.2 at least one of them occurs.

Remarks and open questions. The constant 𝑚0 in our theorems is not effective, and we
do not claim that the exponent 1/3 is optimal. We do not know whether we are in the flat
case or in the contracting case, and we have not shown that the two cases exclude each other
(see the end of Section D). To make 𝑚0 effective along the lines of this proof we would have
to decide which case holds, and in the contracting case we would also need values for the
numbers 𝜇 and 𝑠 of Definition D.1, which the proof of Theorem D.2 does not provide.
   The exponent 1/3 comes from the flat case alone, since the contracting case gives the
stronger bound of Theorem K.7. Two quantities limit the argument in the flat case: the
𝑘 bits that name a balanced set, and the √ scaled exponent 𝑐 𝑔𝜑 (𝑃 ) of the set 𝑃 of all ends,
which Proposition E.9 bounds by about 𝑘. We do not know whether the bound in the flat
case can be improved.

         Appendix M. All fields and the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial
   This appendix proves the lower bound of Theorem L.1 over every field, with a constant
that does not depend on the field (Theorem M.3), and deduces that VP ̸= VNP over every
field (Corollary M.4). Sections A to L use nothing about k except that it is a field, apart
from the element 12 in Proposition E.1, and for uniform constants we apply them to a single
field in each characteristic. Since the permanent is the determinant in characteristic two,
we also treat the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial HC𝑀 , for which Proposition M.2 replaces
Proposition E.1 and needs no 21 .

M.1. Reduction to the algebraic closure of the prime field. Let 𝑝 be zero or a prime,
and let K be the algebraic closure of the prime field of characteristic 𝑝.
Lemma M.1 (circuits with constants in K). Let 𝑓 be a polynomial with integer coefficients.
If 𝑓 has a circuit with 𝐿 gates over some field of characteristic 𝑝, then it has a circuit with
𝐿 gates over K.
Proof. We embed the given field into an algebraically closed field, which contains a copy
of K, and adjoin the constants of the circuit to this copy. That the circuit computes 𝑓 is an
identity in the resulting finitely generated K-algebra, and we reduce it modulo a maximal
ideal, whose residue field is K by the Nullstellensatz.                                     □
                                            VP ̸= VNP                                           61

M.2. Sums over bits as Hamiltonian cycle polynomials. The next proposition is the
analogue of Proposition E.1 for the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial.
Proposition M.2 (sums over bits as Hamiltonian cycle polynomials). Let 𝑅 be a commu-
tative ring, and let the surjections 𝑣 : 𝐼 → 𝑉 and 𝑤 : 𝐼 → 𝐾 and the 2 × 2 matrices 𝑊𝑛
over 𝑅 be as in Proposition E.1. Then the sum of that proposition is HC𝑀 of an 𝑀 × 𝑀
matrix whose entries are 0, 1 and single values 𝑊𝑛 (𝑥, 𝑟), for every 𝑀 ≥ 40|𝐼|.
Proof. We construct a directed graph with weighted edges in which the products of the
weights along the Hamiltonian cycles add up to the given sum, and we take its weighted
adjacency matrix.
Segments and XORs. The graph is a cyclic row of segments, each of which offers a few
parallel options, which are directed paths from one junction to the next. An XOR between
two options consists of three new vertices 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐 with edges in both directions along 𝑎–𝑏–𝑐,
and the first option is led through it from 𝑎 to 𝑐 and the second from 𝑐 to 𝑎. A Hamiltonian
cycle must pass 𝑏 one way or the other, and if it runs 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐 then it follows the first option
from junction to junction, also through several XORs in series. Hence it takes exactly one
of the two options, and every choice with this property gives exactly one cycle. The weight
of an option is put on its first edge, and in each segment at most one option passes no XOR,
so that there are no parallel edges.
The graph. For every element of 𝑉 and of 𝐾 there is a segment with the two options 𝑇 and 𝐹 ,
which stand for the values 1 and 0 of the bits of 𝑥 and of 𝑟. For every 𝑛 ∈ 𝐼 there are seven
further segments, namely the selector with an option 𝑠𝑡 of weight 𝑊𝑛 (𝑡) for each 𝑡 ∈ {𝑇, 𝐹 }2 ,
for each 𝑡 a flag with the options 𝑦𝑡 and 𝑦¯𝑡 , the 𝑥-check with the options 𝜉𝑇 𝑇 , 𝜉𝑇 𝐹 , 𝜉0 , and
the 𝑟-check with the options 𝜌𝑇 𝑇 , 𝜌𝐹 𝑇 , 𝜌0 . The ten XORs at 𝑛 join 𝑦¯𝑡 with 𝑠𝑡 and, where
they exist, with 𝜉𝑡 and 𝜌𝑡 , and they join 𝜉0 and 𝜌0 with the option 𝑇 of the segments of 𝑣(𝑛)
and of 𝑤(𝑛), respectively. Thus 𝑦𝑡 is taken exactly for the one 𝑡 for which 𝑠𝑡 is taken, and
the two checks then force this 𝑡 to be (𝑥𝑣(𝑛) , 𝑟𝑤(𝑛) ). The Hamiltonian cycles thus correspond
                                                             ∏︀
to the pairs (𝑥, 𝑟), and the cycle of (𝑥, 𝑟) has the weight 𝑛 𝑊𝑛 (𝑥𝑣(𝑛) , 𝑟𝑤(𝑛) ).
The size. There are |𝑉 | + |𝐾| + 7|𝐼| junctions and 10|𝐼| XORs, which makes at most 39|𝐼|
vertices, and segments with a single option of weight 1 give every larger 𝑀 .                     □
   With HC𝑀 in place of PERM𝑀 and 40𝑞 in place of 3𝑞, Corollary E.2 therefore holds in
every characteristic, and its entries are again affine in one variable each. We call a family
(𝐺𝑀 ) of polynomials over a field k in 𝑀 × 𝑀 variables complete for grid sums with factor 𝜃 if
the following holds: whenever in Proposition E.1 the ring 𝑅 is a polynomial ring over k, with
or without the element 21 , and the entries of every matrix 𝑊𝑛 are affine in one variable, which
depends on 𝑤(𝑛) only, the sum there is, for every 𝑀 ≥ 𝜃|𝐼|, the value of 𝐺𝑀 at a matrix
whose entries are affine in one variable each. Thus (PERM𝑀 ) has this property with factor
3 if 2 ̸= 0, and (HC𝑀 ) with factor 40. Corollary E.2, Lemma E.3 and with it Theorem E.11
hold for every such family, with 𝜃 in place of 3. These constructions are in the tradition
of Valiant and Malod [Val79, Mal03], and a completeness proof for the Hamiltonian cycle
polynomial along these lines can be found in [FPV13].
M.3. The lower bound over all fields. We can now state the main theorem in its general
form.
Theorem M.3 (lower bound over all fields). There is an absolute constant 𝑐 > 0 such
that for every 𝑝, zero or prime, there is 𝑚0 with the following property. Over every field of
characteristic 𝑝 and for every 𝑚 ≥ 𝑚0 , every circuit for HC𝑚 , and every circuit for PERM𝑚
                                  1/3
if 𝑝 ̸= 2, has more than 𝑚𝑐(log 𝑚) gates.
Proof. By Lemma M.1 it suffices to consider circuits over K, where Theorem D.2 gives the
flat or the contracting case, depending on 𝑝. The constant of Theorem E.11 in the flat case
is absolute, and the bound of Theorem K.7 in the contracting case, although its constant
62                                           CLAUDE

depends on 𝑝, exceeds that of the flat case for all large 𝑚, as in the proof of Theorem L.1.
For HC𝑚 , Proposition M.2 replaces Proposition E.1, which enters through Corollary E.2
in Lemmas E.3 and K.3. There the factor 3 becomes 40, and the proofs only used that
3𝑘 3 ℓ 2𝑘ℓ ≤ 22𝑘ℓ for large values of the parameters.                                    □
     The separation of the classes follows as in Corollary L.3.
Corollary M.4. Over every field, VP is a proper subset of VNP.
Proof. The family (HC𝑚 ) is in VNP [Val79], as one sees by summing over one-hot codes of
the places that the vertices take on the cycle, with vertex 1 at place 1. By Theorem M.3 it
is not in VP.                                                                            □

                          Appendix N. Approximate computation
   In this appendix we extend Theorem M.3 to circuits that only approximate the polynomial,
in the sense of border complexity (Theorem N.5), so that VNP is not contained in the closure
VP of VP. We first reduce to constants that are Laurent polynomials in a formal variable
𝜀 over K, and in the flat case we then only have to adapt Lemma E.7. In the contracting
case an approximating circuit gives a program that is correct only up to multiples of 𝜀, and
Proposition N.4, whose long proof may be skipped at first reading, excludes such programs.
N.1. Approximating circuits.
Definition N.1. Let k be a field, and let k((𝜀)) be the field of formal Laurent series over k.
A circuit over k((𝜀)) approximates a polynomial 𝑓 over k if it computes a polynomial of the
form 𝑓 + 𝜀𝑔, where 𝑔 is a polynomial all of whose coefficients lie in k[[𝜀]]. We then fix one
such polynomial and call it the output of the circuit. The class VP is defined like VP, with
approximating circuits in place of circuits.
  Over an algebraically closed field, 𝑓 is approximated with 𝐿 gates if and only if it lies in
the Zariski closure of the polynomials that have circuits with 𝐿 gates [Ald84, Bür04], but we
do not use this fact.
Lemma N.2 (constants that are Laurent polynomials). Let 𝑓 be a polynomial with integer
coefficients, and let k be a field of characteristic 𝑝. If a circuit with 𝐿 gates over k((𝜀))
approximates 𝑓 , then some circuit with 𝐿 gates over the ring K[𝜀, 𝜀−1 ] computes a polynomial
that lies in 𝑓 + 𝜀 K[𝜀][𝑥].
Proof. We cut every constant off above 𝜀𝑁 with 𝑁 = 2𝐿 Δ, where no constant contains a
power of 𝜀 below 𝜀−Δ . Since the output has degree at most 2𝐿 in the constants, the cut
changes it only in the positive powers of 𝜀. We then apply the argument of Lemma M.1 to
the coefficients of the constants, which are now Laurent polynomials.                □
N.2. Approximating circuits in the flat case. The flat case needs Lemma E.7 for func-
tionals that are Laurent polynomials.
Lemma N.3 (sums with few terms, approximate version). Lemma E.7 remains true if the
functionals of which the 𝑓𝑗 consist have coefficients in k[𝜀, 𝜀−1 ], no negative power of 𝜀 occurs
in 𝑗 𝑓𝑗 (𝑇𝑗 ), and 𝑡 is the coefficient of 𝜀0 in this sum.
   ∑︀

Proof. We follow the proof of Lemma E.7, and let all exponents of 𝜀 in the functionals lie in
[−Δ, Δ]. As no negative powers occur, 𝑡𝑛 is the coefficient of 𝜀0 in the 𝑛th power of the sum,
which is again a sum over the types. For each type the functional at a party outside 𝑃 is a
sum of at most 2𝑛Δ + 1 functionals over k, one for each power of 𝜀, so that this coefficient is
covered by (2𝑛Δ + 1)|𝑉 | copies of the same tensor as before, a subexponential number. □
   It follows that Proposition E.10, and with it Theorem E.11, holds for approximating
circuits. Indeed, we apply Lemma E.4, which holds over every commutative ring, over
k((𝜀)), cut the functionals off at a power of 𝜀 that is high against their negative exponents
times the number of parties, and use Lemma N.3 in place of Lemma E.7.
                                           VP ̸= VNP                                           63

N.3. Approximate accelerators in the contracting case. For the contracting case we
weaken the notion of an accelerator over a commutative 𝐴-algebra 𝑅.
Definition. An approximate accelerator over 𝑅 is defined as in Definition K.1, except that
its constants lie in 𝑅[𝜀, 𝜀−1 ] and that in property (ii) its value on a 𝑣 𝐻 -plain instance 𝑧 is
only required to lie in sum(𝑧) + 𝜀𝑅[𝜀].
  Approximate accelerators of moderate size do not exist either.
Proposition N.4 (no approximate accelerators). Theorem K.6 holds for approximate ac-
celerators.

Formalization note. The proof below describes the programs and accounts for their sizes and
running times to the level of exponents. That the programs do what is claimed is proved
only in the Lean development.
Proof. We carry the given program to larger scales by the rounds of Proposition K.5, keeping
the error a multiple of 𝜀, and at a large scale we extract the coefficient of 𝜀0 .
   Suppose that there is an approximate accelerator of size 𝑣 𝑎 at scale 𝑣 over a nonzero 𝐴-
algebra 𝐸. Let Δ bound the absolute values of the exponents of 𝜀 in its constants, and for
𝑟 ≥ 1 let 𝜎𝑟 be the substitution 𝜀 ↦→ 𝜀𝑟 , an endomorphism of 𝐸[𝜀, 𝜀−1 ]. All constants below
arise from the given ones by such substitutions, so that the 𝑛th power of each of them has
at most 2𝑛Δ + 1 terms. We carry the program to the scales 𝑢 = 𝑣 𝑎+5 , 𝑢5 , 𝑢25 , and so on,
keeping at the scale 𝑢 a program of size at most 𝑢4ℎ0 and a trie of at most 𝑢 such constants.
The height of the trie and the exponent bound are 𝑏0 + 𝑂(lg 𝑢), where 𝑏0 ≤ ((𝑎 + 1) lg 𝑣)𝑂(1)

depends on the first step only, and the exponents of 𝜀 in the constants are at most 2(lg 𝑢)
in absolute value, with a constant in 𝑂(1) that does not depend on Δ for large 𝑢.

the next scale. Let 𝑝 be a program whose     values on plain instances 𝑧 lie in sum(𝑧) + 𝜀𝐸[𝜀].
