of times that this name is to be used, and a number 𝑘 < 2𝛽 . It names the coefficient of the
𝑘th term of 𝑐𝑛 , an element of 𝐸 that is 0 if there is no such term, and adds the exponent
of this term to a counter modulo 2𝑊 . These exponents form a table of 𝑢𝑂(1) numbers in
the program. Then the program runs the interpreter on 𝐷, counting each name against its
number 𝑛, and it accepts if the interpreter does and the counter is 0.

2 in absolute value, that the value of the new program is the coefficient of 𝜀0 in the value
of 𝐷, which is sum(𝑧) on plain instances. The coefficients are at most 𝑢𝑂(1) new constants
with the exponent bound 𝐵 = 1, since 𝑛 and 𝑘 have 𝑂(lg 𝑢) digits. The guesses and the
interpreter increase the running time by a polylogarithmic factor, so that the accepting runs
take at most 𝑢𝐻−16 steps. This is an accelerator over 𝐸 of size 𝑢𝑂(1) with a fixed exponent,
which contradicts Theorem K.6.                                                             □
N.4. The lower bound for approximating circuits. We can now prove the lower bound
for approximating circuits.
Theorem N.5 (approximate computation). Theorem M.3 holds for circuits that approxi-
mate HC𝑚 , respectively PERM𝑚 . In particular VNP ̸⊆ VP over every field.
Proof. By Lemma N.2 we may assume that the constants of the circuit lie in K[𝜀, 𝜀−1 ]. The
flat case was treated after Lemma N.3. In the contracting case the proofs of Lemmas K.2
and K.3 work over this ring and give approximate accelerators. Indeed, the output Φ + 𝜀𝑔 of
the circuit may have a large degree, but the program of Lemma K.2 gives its part of degree
at most 𝑑, which is Φ plus 𝜀 times that part of 𝑔. Proposition N.4 excludes them, with the
calculation of Theorems K.7 and M.3. For the last claim, (HC𝑚 ) is in VNP.               □
Remark N.6 (determinantal complexity). This remark is the one numbered statement of
the paper that has not been formalized in Lean. Since the determinant has circuits of polyno-
mial size [Ber84], Theorem N.5 has the following consequence. Over fields of characteristic
different from two there is 𝑐 > 0 such that, for all large 𝑚, the polynomial PERM𝑚 is
not approximated, in the sense of Definition N.1, by determinants of affine matrices of size
         1/3
𝑚𝑐(log 𝑚) . Over C, and for matrices of polynomial size, this was conjectured by Mulmuley
and Sohoni [MS01] in the language of orbit closures.

we have |𝑧𝛾|2 ≤ (1 − 𝑡)|𝑧𝑥|2 + 𝑡|𝑧𝑣|2 − (1 − 𝑡)|𝛾𝑥|2 − 𝑡|𝛾𝑣|2 , and (1 − 𝑡)|𝛾𝑥|2 + 𝑡|𝛾𝑣|2 ≥

it gives |𝑥𝛾| ≤ 𝑡|𝑥𝑣| and |𝛾𝑣| ≤ (1 − 𝑡)|𝑥𝑣|, and hence equality in both. The uniqueness is the
inequality at a second point 𝑧 with the same distances, for which the right-hand side is 0.
                                                    VP ̸= VNP                                                    65

Part (b). Let 0 < 𝜂 < 1 and let 𝑧 be the point at 𝜂 on [𝑦, 𝑢]. By the triangle inequality and
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we have |𝑥𝑣|2 ≤ |𝑥𝑧|2 /𝜂 + |𝑧𝑣|2 /(1 − 𝜂). We bound |𝑥𝑧|2 and
|𝑧𝑣|2 by (a) and choose 𝜂 = |𝑥𝑦|/(|𝑥𝑦| + |𝑢𝑣|), taking a limit if this is 0 or 1, which gives
the first statement. The second follows because 2|𝑥𝑦||𝑢𝑣| ≤ |𝑥𝑦|2 + |𝑢𝑣|2 . Equality in the
second statement forces |𝑥𝑦| = |𝑢𝑣|, and likewise |𝑥𝑢| = |𝑦𝑣| when the roles of 𝑦 and 𝑢 are
exchanged.
Parts (c) and (d). The barycenter of (c), with all weights equal to 1, and the nearest point of
(d) are minimizers as in the first step, and the inequalities of (c) and (d) are (O.1), which also
shows that there is only one minimizer. For the last statement of (c) we add the inequality
of (c) for the points 𝑥𝑖 at 𝑦 = 𝑏′ and for the points 𝑥′𝑖 at 𝑦 = 𝑏, ∑︀        and we apply (b) to the four
points 𝑏′ , 𝑥𝑖 , 𝑏, 𝑥′𝑖 for every 𝑖, which together give 𝑛|𝑏𝑏′ |2 ≤ |𝑏𝑏′ | 𝑖 |𝑥𝑖 𝑥′𝑖 |.
Part (e). The product is complete, midpoints are taken coordinate by coordinate, and the
CN inequality for the product is the sum of those for the coordinates.
Part (f). Let Φ(𝑝, 𝑝′ ; 𝑟, 𝑟′ ) = |𝑝𝑟′ |2 + |𝑝′ 𝑟|2 − |𝑝𝑟|2 − |𝑝′ 𝑟′ |2 , so that Φ(𝑝, 𝑝′ ; 𝑟, 𝑟′ ) ≤ 2|𝑝𝑝′ ||𝑟𝑟′ |
by (b). We have Φ(𝑥, 𝑢; 𝑦, 𝑣) = Φ(𝑥, 𝑎; 𝑦, 𝑏) + Φ(𝑥, 𝑎; 𝑏, 𝑣) + Φ(𝑎, 𝑢; 𝑦, 𝑏) + Φ(𝑎, 𝑢; 𝑏, 𝑣), and
the bounds for the four terms add up to 2|𝑥𝑢||𝑦𝑣| = 2ℎ2 , which by the hypotheses is the
value of Φ(𝑥, 𝑢; 𝑦, 𝑣). So all four bounds are equalities, and the first one says that
(O.2)                               |𝑥𝑏|2 + |𝑦𝑎|2 − 𝛿 2 − |𝑎𝑏|2 = 2𝑠𝑡ℎ2 .
Let first 𝑠 = 𝑡. By (b) we have |𝑥𝑏|2 + |𝑦𝑎|2 ≤ |𝑥𝑎|2 + |𝑦𝑏|2 + 2|𝑥𝑦||𝑎𝑏| = 2𝑠2 ℎ2 + 2𝛿|𝑎𝑏|, so
that (|𝑎𝑏| − 𝛿)2 ≤ 0 and |𝑥𝑏|2 + |𝑦𝑎|2 = 2𝛿 2 + 2𝑠2 ℎ2 . By (a) both |𝑥𝑏|2 and |𝑦𝑎|2 are at most
𝛿 2 + 𝑠2 ℎ2 , so both are equal to it. As 𝑏 does not depend on 𝑠 and 𝑎 does not depend on 𝑡, it
follows that |𝑥𝑏|2 = 𝛿 2 + 𝑡2 ℎ2 and |𝑦𝑎|2 = 𝛿 2 + 𝑠2 ℎ2 for all 𝑠 and 𝑡, and inserting this into
(O.2) gives the claim.                                                                         □

                          Appendix Q. The Lean development
  This appendix describes the formalization on which Section 3 relies. The file of definitions
and statements is printed in Section 3. Here we say how it can be checked, list the Lean
theorem that belongs to each numbered statement of the paper, and say where the Lean
development differs from the text.
  The development uses Lean 4.33.1 [Lean] and Mathlib [Mathlib] at commit
                       0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474.
The file printed in Section 3 imports Mathlib and nothing else, and it is printed as it
stands in the development, without the license comment of five lines at its head. It
defines circuits, the two polynomials, approximating circuits and Valiant’s classes, and
it states four propositions: MainTheorem is Theorem M.3, MainTheoremApprox is the
first assertion of Theorem N.5, Separation is Corollary M.4, and SeparationClosure
is the second assertion of Theorem N.5. In the namespace Solution the theorems
mainTheorem, mainTheoremApprox, separation and separationClosure have exactly
the types MainTheorem, MainTheoremApprox, Separation and SeparationClosure, and
permanent_over_real is Theorem L.1.

Q.1. Checks of the development. The folder https://github.com/anthropics/formal-math/
tree/main/vp-vnp builds with Lean 4.33.1 and Mathlib’s compiled files. It uses no
native_decide and declares no axioms, and its only uses of sorry are in Challenge.lean,
which states the theorems of Solution without proof so that the tool Comparator can com-
pare against them. The theorems of Solution depend on no axioms other than propext,
Classical.choice and Quot.sound, as scripts/PrintAxioms.lean prints. Comparator
accepted them against Challenge.lean, rechecking the proofs with Lean’s kernel and with
the independently written checker nanoda. The file printed in Section 3 is Statement.lean,
without the license comment at its head.
  Whether the statements say what is meant was judged only by AI readers. No human
mathematician has checked the development, the statement file or the proofs.

Q.2. The numbered statements in Lean. Every numbered statement of the paper except
Remark N.6 has a Lean theorem. Table 3 lists them in the order of the paper, and for some
statements it names a second Lean theorem; it also lists the display (F.1), and eq_Q is the
display (B.2).

Q.3. Differences between the text and the Lean development. The theorems with
prefix Paper. state the numbered statements, and each of them is proved once. The theorems
of Solution are proved through them, with these exceptions, which are not on that route:
Theorem L.2, Corollary L.3, the qualitative bound in the flat case (Proposition E.5 and
Theorem E.6) and the definitions. Many unnumbered steps of the printed proofs have named
theorems too. Lean does not identify equivalent tensors (def_spectral_congr shows that
spectral points respect the identifications), and it writes the standing hypotheses out. Some
theorems hold there for every field, or under weaker hypotheses. The theorems with prefix
Paper. are stated for fields in the lowest universe, and so are their rings, algebras, groups,
spaces, point sets and index sets. The exception is the given field in Theorems M.3 and N.5,
Corollary M.4, Definition N.1 and Lemmas M.1 and N.2, which may lie in any universe. This
restricts the printed statements to the lowest universe. The theorems of Solution hold for
fields in every universe.
   The Lean development uses some other words. It says gauge for an upper functional, unit
for a packet, table for a node tensor, bill for a tally, near pair for an edge of the Cayley graph

Statements. The statements differ in the following points.
   ∙ The numbers 𝐶, 𝑎 and 𝛼 are natural numbers.
   ∙ In the file printed in Section 3 circuits and the classes VP      and VNP are defined over
                                                                     ∑︀ ∏︀
     commutative semirings. The permanent is written there as 𝜎 𝑖 𝑥𝜎(𝑖),𝑖 , where Section 1
     has 𝑥𝑖,𝜎(𝑖) ; the two sums agree, as one sees by replacing 𝜎 with its inverse.
   ∙ Lean defines the projection to the fixed points of a window (Section F) as the barycenter
     of the orbit, and projection_nearest shows that this is the nearest point. In the
     proof of Theorem F.6 the space of functions carries the sum where the text has the
     mean, which is one scaling factor. Lean states Lemma F.3 for explicit numbers, 𝜗𝑛 =
     18⌈log2 𝑛⌉ 𝜇(𝐿 + 1)𝑠𝐿 with 𝐿 = ⌊log𝜇 2⌈log2 𝑛⌉ ⌋ (kappa), which are not those of the
     printed proof, and 𝐶(𝑛) in (F.1) and in Lemmas F.4 and F.5 is formed with them.
   ∙ The families of tensors in Theorem A.6 and of maps in Proposition A.7 are indexed by
     any finite set.
   ∙ A variable of block 𝑎 of 𝑓𝑘,ℓ is indexed by 𝑘 strings, and the one at the place 𝑎 is 0 in
     the variables that occur. The circuit sizes are the same, and the theorems with suffix
     _printed state Lemma E.3 and Propositions E.5 and E.10 for the printed polynomial.
   ∙ Lemmas A.3, E.4, E.7, F.1, F.2, I.2, K.3, M.1, N.2 and N.3 and Proposition B.2 are
     proved in stronger or more general forms. For example, lem_hubs pushes the tensor
     𝑡 forward along an arbitrary map from its parties to a finite set of parties, of which
     𝑜1 , 𝑜2 are any two, and lem_hubs_further is the form with two further parties. Propo-
     sition B.2 is proved without the assumption that Σ1 and Σ2 are nonempty. Lemma E.4
     is proved over every commutative semiring, and Lemmas E.7 and N.3 whenever 𝑡 is
     covered by 𝑇1 , . . . , 𝑇𝐽 , so that linear maps may also be applied at the parties of 𝑃 . In
     Section F Lemma F.1 is proved without the assumption 𝑠2 < 𝜇 and also for 𝑟 = 0, and
     Lemma F.2 without the conditions 1 ≤ 𝑘 < 𝑛 and 𝜉, 𝜔 ̸= 0. Lemma K.3 is proved for
     every family that is complete for grid sums (Appendix M) and for every 𝐻 ≥ 32. Lem-
     mas M.1 and N.2 are proved with any algebraically closed field of characteristic 𝑝 in the
     place of K. The flat bound for approximating circuits has 14 where Proposition E.10
     has 16.
   ∙ Laws may have any finite sets of elements, slots and channels. A law in Lean also
     records that its channels connect its elements, in the form that indices which agree
     along all channels agree everywhere; on 𝑈ℎ , where the channels are all the edges, this
     holds by itself. The conditions on 𝜆, on ℐ and on 𝑈ℎ come with Theorem G.2. Its Lean
     form also states the choices of Section G: 𝑢 and 𝐶0 are as in Theorem F.6, 𝐶0′ ≥ 2𝐶0 + 1
     is an integer and 𝐶1 = 4𝐶0′ . It asserts that some multiple ℎ ≥ 2𝑢 of 𝑢 satisfies (G.1),
     and it asserts (a) and (b) for every such ℎ. That the channels there are the edges, each
     once, on which the norm in Theorem G.2(b) depends, follows from the definitions but
     is stated by no theorem of the development. The theorem def_law lists only three of
     the fields of the structure, in one direction, so that it rests on reading the structure that
     it is the printed notion.
   ∙ In Proposition H.1, the set 𝑉0 is only stated to have as many elements as 𝐺𝑛 , bijectivity
     on cosets is stated as equivariance and freeness, and the cosets over a coset are given
     by representatives (prop_tower_representatives: there is exactly one in each coset).
     Lean states the proposition for every ℎ ≥ 3 and every 𝑛 ≥ 0.
   ∙ Theorem H.2 and Proposition J.2 state their clauses as the fields of structures. A relay
     is a law with one slot, two channels, one index and 𝜆 = 0, applied to a batch of packets.
     Thus relays too have outcomes and leave copies and dead packets at the ends of their
     strings, an outcome of a relay is good if it equals the number of packets of the batch
     on both channels, and there is a law move for every stage, type, coset and law, with
                                       VP ̸= VNP                                         69

  𝑡 = 0 for those not chosen. The value of a tree is written as a sum, over the contents
  of named packets, present or absent, of products of the values of its nodes, and no
  theorem restates this as the value of a network as defined in Section H. As printed in
  (b) and (e), the dead packets are a set of names that are not all stated to be present, so
  that the bound on what a node ever holds may count absent ones. The end packets of
  relays count as dead, and in networks without these packets they would count among
  the absent ones. In part (e) Lean has the constant 𝐶 of the theorem, and it counts for
  every move at 𝑝, with the number 𝑡, also 𝑡 times one plus the number of its slots at 𝑝,
  which covers the zeros in slots. Proposition J.2 uses only the total 𝑀 𝐶 . Packets have a
  direction in Lean, and packets from a node to itself are allowed and stay at their node.
  The programs may depend on 𝑀 , 𝑁 , ℓ, the ring of the network and the bound on the
  number of given nodes, respectively on 𝑣, 𝑇 , 𝑘, the automaton and the standing data.
  In Proposition J.2 the outcomes range over tuples of numbers below a bound that is
  part of the data (the field U1); no clause says that every good outcome lies below it,
  and the identity sums over those that do. Whether an outcome is good is tested with
  the integer ⌊2𝐶1 𝑎21 ⌋ (thm_tree_good). This implies the printed notion and is equal to
  it when 2𝐶1 𝑎21 is an integer, as the laws provide.
∙ Proposition J.2 is proved without the bound on lg 𝑇 , and its number of bits in all counts
  every copy ever held and the zeros in slots. Its sum runs over the numbers of packets
  with at most 𝑁 packets at each node, as in the printed proof, where Lean takes for 𝑁
  the last bound of Lemma I.2, it is stated for every pair of a first and a last state, and
  the exponent of Λ is the constant 𝐶 of the statement. It also states what the program
  needs besides: the packets have at most 𝑇 bits, at most 𝑇 Λ bits start at a node, and
  the numbers of the program have room for packets, outcomes and pieces.

∙ Proposition K.5, Theorem K.6 and Proposition N.4 do not assume the contracting case.

∙ In Section I.1, Lean reads the construct of a pair from the length of its first component

     runs, and halting is stated for the 𝑧 with |𝑧| < 𝑛. In Lemma K.3, lem_circuit_phi
     states what is used of Φ, not that it equals the printed sum over words.
Routes of proof. The theorems of the main route follow the references between the printed
proofs, with these exceptions.
    ∙ In Proposition K.5, Theorem K.6 and Proposition N.4 some printed references are to
      definitions or enter as standing hypotheses. Proposition N.4 repeats the iteration of
      Proposition K.5, as printed.
    ∙ Lemma K.3, Theorem M.3 and Theorem N.5 use the general forms for families that are
      complete for grid sums, of which Corollary E.2 and the statements about the permanent
      are instances.
    ∙ Proposition E.10 uses the step prop_flat_lower_sum of the proof of Proposition E.5,
      not the proposition. Lemma N.2 repeats the argument of Lemma M.1 on coefficients.
    ∙ Theorem N.5 does not cite Lemma K.3, Theorem K.7 and Theorem M.3. Instead it
      uses the step thm_allfields_exceeds of the proof of the last of them and approxi-
      mate analogues of the first two, which share their steps with them; the analogues are
      lem_circuit_approx and contracting_approx_complete. On the way the constants
      are cut off twice.
    ∙ Of Lemma A.2 only the clauses that say that 𝑑𝜑 is a pseudometric are taken from the
      lemma: the power law and 𝑑𝜑 ≤ 1 come from its named parts, and the bound by a
      tree is used nowhere. The clause of Lemma K.4 on the size is never cited and is proved
      again where it is needed. Part (d) of Lemma C.4 is redundant where it is used. The
      form of the constants in Lemma N.2 is derived again in the proof of Theorem N.5.
    ∙ Theorem H.2 uses parts (a) and (b) of Proposition H.1 through the named theorems
      that also prove the proposition.
The proofs of Proposition A.5 and Theorem A.6 are carried out in a commutative monoid
with a covering relation, which is then taken to be the classes of tensors. In the proof of
Theorem A.6 the certificate is first raised by one level, and the number of terms is bounded
by (𝑐 + 1)𝑘+1 𝜂 −𝑁 . Some steps exist only under names of the underlying library and not as
theorems with prefix Paper. These are the sorting pass, the tape encoding and the time
count of Proposition I.1, the constructions of Propositions E.1 and M.2, the induction of
Lemma E.4, the word in Lemma F.2, the averaging in Lemma F.1, the properties of the
mean E in Section C, and the semantics of scripts inside the evaluator. Lean proofs can be
coarser or more general at single steps. The proof of Theorem G.2, for example, uses all
loads of bounded norm where the text uses the rounded ones, and the proof of Theorem H.2
uses (G.1) as it stands where the printed proof uses only 1 + 4𝐶1 ≤ ℎ/4.
Not in Lean. Remark N.6, the example of flattening ranks after Definition A.1, the remarks
that follow the proof of Theorem D.2, the fact that 𝑟 in the proof of Proposition A.5 is also
a limit, and the factor three between the two ways of counting gates in Section 1 are not
formalized.
   Comments in the Lean files are informal: some paraphrase the paper, say a little more than
the step that they name proves, or describe a bound or a definition loosely. The theorems
follow the present text, up to the differences listed.
